Improved well-posedness for quasilinear and sharp local well-posedness for semilinear KP-I equations Thanks: *Corresponding author.
Shinya Kinoshita, Akansha Sanwal, Robert Schippa*

Abstract
. We show new well-posedness results in anisotropic Sobolev spaces for dispersion-generalized KP-I equations with increased dispersion compared to the KP-I equation. We obtain the sharp dispersion rate, below which generalized KP-I equations on ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} and on ℝ × 𝕋 \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T} exhibit quasilinear behavior. In the quasilinear regime, we show improved well-posedness results relying on short-time Fourier restriction. In the semilinear regime, we show sharp well-posedness with analytic data-to-solution mapping. On ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} we cover the full subcritical range, whereas on ℝ × 𝕋 \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T} the sharp well-posedness is strictly subcritical. Nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequalities are one ingredient. These are presently proved for Borel measures with growth condition reflecting the different geometries of the plane ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} , the cylinder ℝ × 𝕋 \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T} , and the torus 𝕋 2 \mathbb{T}^{2} . Finally, we point out that on tori 𝕋 γ 2 \mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma} , KP-I equations are never semilinear.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Dispersion-generalized KP-I equations

In this article, we show new low regularity well-posedness for the dispersion-generalized Kadomtsev Petviashvili-I (KP-I) equations:

(1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}u-\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u-\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}^{2}u&=u\partial_{x}u,\quad(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D},\\
u(0)&=u_{0},\end{array}\right.

where
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{K}
,
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})\}
, and
\alpha\geqslant 2
. The dual variables of
(t,x,y)
are denoted by
(\tau,\xi,\eta)
. We define
\partial_{x}^{-1}
and
D_{x}^{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{D}
as Fourier multiplier

\widehat{(\partial_{x}^{-1}f)}(\xi,\eta)=(i\xi)^{-1}\hat{f}(\xi,\eta),\quad\widehat{(D_{x}^{\alpha}f)}(\xi,\eta)=|\xi|^{\alpha}\hat{f}(\xi,\eta).

By local well-posedness we refer to existence, uniqueness, and continuity of the data-to-solution mapping assigning initial data from suitable Sobolev spaces to continuous curves on time intervals

S_{T}:H^{s,0}(\mathbb{D})\to X_{T}\hookrightarrow C([0,T],H^{s,0}(\mathbb{D})),\quad u_{0}\mapsto u

with
T=T(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s,0}})
depending lower semicontinuously on the norm of the initial data and
T(u)\gtrsim 1
for
u\downarrow 0
. We refer to the evolution as
semilinear
if the solution mapping is real-analytic. This will be the case when the solution mapping is constructed via Picard iteration as a consequence of real analyticity of the nonlinearity. The evolution is referred to as
quasilinear
if the data-to-solution mapping fails to be real analytic.

For
\alpha=2
, (
1
) becomes the original KP-I equation. The KP equations were introduced by Kadomtsev and Petviashvili to model two-dimensional water waves (see
[
22
]
and
[
1
]
) with an emphasis on the transverse stability of solitons of the Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) equation:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u=u\partial_{x}u.

The KdV equation is a one-dimensional model of traveling waves in shallow water. Adding the weakly transverse approximation of the wave dispersion relation

\omega_{\Box}(\xi,\eta)=\pm(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}=\pm\xi\Big(1+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\xi^{2}}\Big)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\eta^{4}}{\xi^{3}}\Big),\quad\xi>0

to the KdV equation results in the KP evolution after changing the system of rest and mild dilation:

(2)

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u+\kappa\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}^{2}u=u\partial_{x}u,\quad\kappa\in\{1;-1\}.

For
\kappa=-1
the equation is known as KP-I equation, and for
\kappa=1
the equation is referred to as KP-II.
Similarly, starting from dispersion-generalized versions of the KdV equation

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u=u\partial_{x}u,\quad\alpha\geqslant 2,

adding the weakly transverse perturbation leads to the dispersion-generalized KP equations:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u+\kappa\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}^{2}u=u\partial_{x}u.

In this paper we are only concerned with the analysis of the dispersion-generalized KP-I equations. The dispersion relation in this case is given by

\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)=\xi|\xi|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{\xi}.

The behavior of solutions to KP-II equations deviates significantly due to a nonlinear defocusing effect absent in the KP-I case, see below.

On
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we observe that for any solution
u
to (
1
) with initial data
\phi
the rescalings

u_{\lambda}(t,x,y)=\lambda^{-\alpha}u(\lambda^{-(\alpha+1)}t,\lambda^{-1}x,\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha+2}{2}}y)

solve (
1
) with initial data

\phi_{\lambda}(x,y)=\lambda^{-\alpha}\phi(\lambda^{-1}x,\lambda^{-\frac{\alpha+2}{2}}y).

Let
\dot{H}^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denote the anisotropic homogeneous Sobolev space with norm

\|f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\xi|^{2s_{1}}|\eta|^{2s_{2}}|\hat{f}(\xi,\eta)|^{2}d\xi d\eta.

We compute

\|\phi_{\lambda}\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\lambda^{-\frac{3\alpha}{4}+1-s_{1}-\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1\big)s_{2}}\|\phi\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

In the scale of the anisotropic Sobolev spaces with
s_{2}=0
, this distinguishes
\dot{H}^{s_{c},0}
with
s_{c}=1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}
as scaling critical space. Moreover,
s_{2}=0
is distinguished as this is the lowest regularity in the
y
-variable, which still respects the Galilean invariance:

\eta\to\eta+A\xi.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we have the following conserved quantities for real-valued solutions:

(3)

\displaystyle M(u)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{D}}u^{2}(x,y)dxdy,

(4)

\displaystyle E_{\alpha}(u)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{D}}\big(\frac{1}{2}|D_{x}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{6}u^{3}\big)dxdy.

The energy space is given by

E^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(\mathbb{D})=\Big\{\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{D}):\Big\|\big(1+|\xi|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}+\frac{|\eta|}{|\xi|}\big)\hat{\phi}(\xi,\eta)\Big\|_{L^{2}}<\infty\Big\}.

Since the energy spaces are smaller spaces than the anisotropic Sobolev spaces, we opt to work in the anisotropic Sobolev spaces
H^{s,0}
, which are defined by

H^{s,0}(\mathbb{D})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{D}):\|\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\hat{f}(\xi,\eta)\|_{L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}<\infty\}.

Both KP equations (
2
) are known to be completely integrable, as they admit a Lax pair (
[
11
]
). Recently, Killip-Vişan
[
23
]
introduced the “method of commuting flows” taking advantage of the complete integrability to obtain sharp global well-posedness in standard Sobolev spaces for one-dimensional models (see also preceding works
[
24
]
and Koch–Tataru
[
28
]
). However, to the best of the authors’ knowledge, these arguments could not be extended so far to models in higher dimensions, like the KP equations. Moreover, the dispersion-generalized models are not known to be completely integrable.

1.2.
Nonlinear evolution: Resonance and transversality considerations

The resonance relation is given by

\begin{split}\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{2})&=\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})-\omega(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})-\omega(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})\\
&=\underbrace{(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}-\xi_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}-\xi_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}}_{\Omega_{\alpha,1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}-\frac{(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})^{2}}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}.\end{split}

For KP-I equations we need to consider the possibility

(5)

|\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{2})|\ll|\Omega_{\alpha,1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})|,

and consequently, we cannot recover the derivative loss in a High
\times
Low-interaction (with regard to the
x
-frequencies) through the resonance. Indeed, Molinet–Saut–Tzvetkov
[
30
]
showed that the data-to-solution mapping of the KP-I equation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is not in
C^{2}
in any anisotropic Sobolev space. They proved global well-posedness in the second energy space in
[
29
]
.

Ionescu–Kenig–Tataru
[
21
]
showed global well-posedness in the first energy space in a seminal contribution, combining Fourier restriction analysis originating from Bourgain’s work (see
[
8
]
and references therein) and the energy method (see Koch–Tzvetkov
[
26
]
for a preceding work combining frequency-dependent time localization and Strichartz estimates). They analyze the nonlinear interaction on frequency-dependent time intervals. Our analysis in the quasilinear case rests likewise on short-time Fourier restriction. We briefly explain our implementation of short-time Fourier restriction on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
(the choice of frequency-dependent time localization depends on the domain) in Subsection
1.5
.

We shall see that in case (
5
) holds, we have

\Big|\frac{(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})^{2}}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}\Big|\sim\big|\Omega_{\alpha,1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big|,

and we obtain favorable bounds for the full transversality. This allows us to obtain trilinear smoothing estimates as consequence of nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequalities, on which we elaborate in Section
1.3
. The trilinear smoothing estimate for the KP-I equation was the second key ingredient in
[
21
]
.

Further tools to control the nonlinear evolution are linear and bilinear Strichartz estimates, which improve on frequency-dependent time intervals. The basic bilinear Strichartz estimates follow from transversality arguments (see Proposition
4.1
, Lemma
4.3
), which are standard by now. We shall see that in case of small transversality we can use a variant of the Córdoba–Fefferman square function estimate, which leads to refined bilinear Strichartz estimates (see Lemma
4.4
).

We show linear Strichartz estimates using
\ell^{2}
-decoupling for elliptic hypersurfaces due to Bourgain–Demeter
[
9
]
. Increasing the dispersion parameter
\alpha
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
,
\ell^{2}
-decoupling captures a smoothing effect, which is not observed in the fully periodic case.
Increasing
\alpha
the resonance relation and the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality become likewise more favorable and on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
, the evolution becomes semilinear for
\alpha
large enough. This is described in detail in Subsection
1.4
.

We remark that the preceding description to control the nonlinear evolution does not depend on the underlying geometry
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
. It is one purpose of the present work to emphasize robust perturbative methods to control nonlinear interactions and to compare the influence of the different geometries on the (multi)linear Strichartz estimates, which are presently described in a unified way.

Lastly, we remark on KP-II equations. The resonance relation for KP-II equations is given by

\Omega^{(II)}_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{2})=\Omega_{\alpha,1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})+\frac{(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})^{2}}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}

and observing that both terms are of the same sign, it follows that

|\Omega^{(II)}_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{2})|\gtrsim|\Omega_{\alpha,1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})|.

This reflects the aforementioned nonlinear defocusing effect. This was pointed out by Bourgain
[
8
]
who showed global well-posedness of the KP-II equation for real-valued solutions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Global well-posedness and scattering for small initial data in the scaling-critical space
\dot{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
was proved by Hadac–Herr–Koch
[
18
]
. In the recent work
[
20
]
Bourgain’s
L^{2}
-well-posedness on the torus is extended to some Sobolev space of negative order. The arguments similarly rely on Strichartz estimates from decoupling and frequency-dependent time localization.

1.3.
Nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequalities with general measure

The nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequalities are convolution inequalities for functions supported on the Pontryagin dual

\mathbb{D}^{*}=(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{K}_{1}\times\mathbb{K}_{2})^{*}\text{ with }\mathbb{R}^{*}=\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\mathbb{T}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}.

The literature on Loomis–Whitney inequalities and the related Brascamp–Lieb inequalities is vast, and the following list of references is by no means exhaustive. We refer the interested reader to the references therein (
[
6
,
2
,
4
,
25
]
) for further reading and to the recent overview by Bennett–Bez
[
5
]
.
However, we remark that most statements in the literature are local and not immediately suitable for application to PDE since the “small” support assumptions on the involved functions are not quantified.
We shall be brief here and refer to Subsection
4.2
for precise notions.

Let
(S_{i})_{i=1}^{3}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
denote
C^{1,\beta}
-hypersurfaces,
\beta>0
, which allow for a global graph parametrization. For
x_{i}\in S_{i}
we denote with
\mathfrak{n}_{i}(x_{i})
the outer unit normal. We suppose that there is
A\geqslant 1
such that for any
x_{i}\in S_{i}
:

(6)

A^{-1}\leqslant|\mathfrak{n}_{1}(x_{1})\wedge\mathfrak{n}_{2}(x_{2})\wedge\mathfrak{n}_{3}(x_{3})|\leqslant 1.

For the (classical) nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequality we endow
S_{i}
with the surface measure. Let
f_{i}:S_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2,3
with
S_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
like above.
S_{i}
carries the surface measure
\sigma_{i}
, and the global convolution estimate proved in
[
25
]
reads as follows:

\int_{S_{3}}(f_{1}*f_{2})f_{3}d\sigma_{3}\lesssim A^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}(S_{i})}.

For our purposes when applying the convolution estimates, we shall thicken the hypersurfaces and consider functions
f_{i}\in L^{2}(S_{i}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})
, which are supported on the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood
S_{i}(\varepsilon)
of
S_{i}
, where
\nu_{\gamma}
is a Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which satisfies for any
r>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
the estimate:

(7)

\nu_{\gamma}(B(x,r))\leqslant C_{\nu}r^{\gamma}\text{ for }0<r\leqslant\varepsilon.

We formulate following version of global nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequalities, which is related to convolution estimates for functions on product spaces.

Theorem 1.1

(Nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequalities with general measure)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
(S_{i})_{i=1,2,3}
be a collection of hypersurfaces, which satisfy Assumption
4.7
,
\nu_{\gamma}
be a Borel measure, which satisfies (
7
), and
f_{i}\in L^{2}(S_{i}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})
,
i=1,2
. Then the following estimate holds:

(8)

\|f_{1}*f_{2}\|_{L^{2}(S_{3}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}\lesssim A^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}(S_{i}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}.

Its proof uses almost orthogonal decompositions like in
[
25
]
. Interestingly, the constant in the estimate (
8
) does not depend on the regularity parameters
b
,
\beta
of
(S_{i})_{i=1,2,3}
from Assumption
4.7
, but it depends only on the constant
C_{\nu}
from (
7
).

1.4.
Improved local well-posedness results

Here we state the new results concerning the well-posedness of KP-I equations. We emphasize that the arguments described above
per se
do not depend on the geometry, but the constants in the linear and multilinear estimates do.

1.4.1.
Euclidean geometry

On
\mathbb{R}^{2}
Z. Guo
et al.

[
16
]
applied the argument of Ionescu–Kenig–Tataru
[
21
]
to show local well-posedness of the KP-I equation in
H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. This is a significantly larger space than the energy space
E^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
considered in
[
21
]
. The second and third author considered generalized KP equations (
1
) for
\alpha\geqslant 2
in
[
33
]
. The analysis combined the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality, bilinear Strichartz estimates and frequency dependent time localization in the quasilinear regime. In the present work we bring improved bilinear Strichartz estimates into the mix recorded in Lemma
4.4
, which take advantage of an orthogonality in case of low transversality.
The argument is reminiscent of the
Córdoba–Fefferman square function estimate
(cf.
[
12
,
10
]
). Estimating differences of solutions at negative Sobolev regularity with a suitable low frequency weight further improves the local well-posedness results.

We summarize the presently obtained results refining the arguments from
[
33
]
:

•

We obtain the sharp dispersion parameter
\alpha_{c}=\frac{5}{2}
such that (
1
) evolves in a semilinear way on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We show that the data-to-solution mapping fails to be
C^{2}
for
\alpha<\alpha_{c}
in Theorem
5.2
.

•

In the semilinear regime
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{c}
we show local well-posedness in the full subcritical range in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
s>1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}
in Theorem
9.1
. This improves
[
33
, Theorem 1.3]
.

•

In the quasilinear case we improve the results in
[
33
]
in Theorem
7.1
.

•

The present argument shows local well-posedness for the KP-I equation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
; see Theorem
6.1
. Shortly before announcing our work, this was independently reported by Guo
[
13
]
.

Remark 1.2
.

We remark that in 04/2024 Guo–Molinet
[
14
]
reported unconditional
1
1

1

This means that solutions are constructed in
C_{T}H^{s,0}
without intersecting with a smaller function space.
global well-posedness in the energy space and local well-posedness in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
s>\frac{3}{4}
. Moreover, shortly before our results were announced in 08/2024, Guo
[
13
]
independently reported local well-posedness of the KP-I equation on Euclidean space for
s>\frac{1}{2}
. Guo uses the same frequency-dependent time localization, but does not employ weighted spaces for the differences of solutions. To compare with our analysis in the dispersion-generalized case, we opted to include the details as well for the KP-I equation in Section
7
.

1.4.2.
Partially periodic geometry

On
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
, T. Robert
[
32
]
showed global well-posedness of the KP-I equation in the energy space and as a consequence stability of the line soliton. Here we show improved local well-posedness in the larger anisotropic Sobolev spaces for the dispersion-generalized equations. We remark that the present analysis yields global well-posedness in the energy space. Indeed, with the (improved) Strichartz estimates and Loomis-Whitney inequality for higher dispersion at hand, this is a direct consequence of the analysis in
[
32
]
. We take the following global result for granted:

Theorem 1.3

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
\alpha\geqslant 2
. Then (
1
) posed on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
is globally well-posed in
E^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
.

This facilitates our analysis in anisotropic Sobolev spaces because we can work with global solutions from the energy space. The data-to-solution mapping will then be extended to the larger space
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{D})
.

Firstly, we obtain the sharp dispersion parameter
\alpha_{c}=5
for semilinear well-posedness. For
\alpha<\alpha_{c}
we show that (
1
) fails to be
C^{2}
-wellposed in anisotropic Sobolev spaces
H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
, and so it is not possible to solve it via the contraction mapping principle. This is recorded in Theorem
5.3
.

To state our improved local well-posedness results, define

(9)

s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})=\begin{cases}\frac{11}{6}-\frac{2\alpha}{3}+\varepsilon,&\quad 2<\alpha\leqslant\frac{11}{4}\text{ and }\varepsilon>0,\\
0,&\quad\frac{11}{4}<\alpha\leqslant 5.\end{cases}

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\alpha\in(2,5)
. The Cauchy problem (
1
) posed on
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
is locally well-posed in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{D})
for
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{D})
for
\varepsilon>0
.

Arguing that the evolution is semilinear for
\alpha\geqslant\alpha_{c}
is more delicate than the example in Theorem
5.3
and based on combining the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality with bilinear Strichartz estimates. The following arguments should only be understood morally. In the resonant interaction for
N_{2}\ll N\sim N_{1}
we find for
T\sim 1
:

\begin{split}&\quad\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(S_{5}(t)P_{N_{1}}u_{0}S_{5}(t)P_{N_{2}}u_{0})\|_{L_{t}^{1}([0,T],L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}\\
&\lesssim N_{1}\big(\frac{N_{1}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}^{-\frac{5}{2}}\|P_{N_{1}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\|P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|P_{N_{1}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\|P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

This shows that the estimate is favorable for
N_{2}\gtrsim N_{1}^{-2}
. On the other hand for
N_{2}\ll N_{1}^{-2}
, we find in the resonant case by a bilinear Strichartz estimate

\begin{split}\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(S_{5}(t)P_{N_{1}}u_{0}S_{5}(t)P_{N_{2}}u_{0})\|_{L^{1}([0,T],L^{2}_{x})}&\lesssim N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}\|P_{N_{1}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\|P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\|P_{N_{1}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\|P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

The above line of argument indicates semilinear global well-posedness of (
1
) for
\alpha\geqslant 5
. We obtain local well-posedness sharp up to endpoints. Notably, the sharp regularity for local well-posedness is strictly above the scaling critical regularity:

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\alpha\geqslant 5
,
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
, and
s^{*}(\alpha)=\frac{1-\alpha}{4}
. (
1
) is locally well-posed for complex valued initial data in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{D})
for
s>s^{*}(\alpha)
. For real-valued initial data (
1
) is globally well-posed in
L^{2}(\mathbb{D})
. Moreover, (
1
) is ill-posed in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
for
s<s^{*}(\alpha)
.

1.5.
Short-time Fourier restriction

Finally, we sketch the proof of local well-posedness in the quasilinear case via short-time Fourier restriction. We shall be brief in the following since the argument from
[
21
]
is standard by now (see also
[
34
]
for a detailed introduction). For definiteness we consider only the partially periodic domain
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
. We make use of a function space
F^{s}(T)
which measures the Fourier restriction norm after frequency-dependent time localization. This is accompanied by a “dual” space
\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)
, which captures the nonlinearity. A consequence of the frequency-dependent time localization is the necessity to introduce an energy space
E^{s}(T)
, which takes into account the
C_{T}H^{s,0}
norm of dyadic frequency ranges separately.

We show the following set of estimates for solutions to (
1
) on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
:

(10)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\|u\|_{F^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|u\|_{E^{s}(T)}+\|\partial_{x}(u^{2})\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)},\\
\|\partial_{x}(u^{2})\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)}&\lesssim T^{\delta(\alpha)}\|u\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)},\\
\|u\|^{2}_{E^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s,0}}^{2}+T^{\delta}\|u\|^{3}_{F^{s}(T)}\end{array}\right.

provided that
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
and some
\delta(\alpha)>0
,
\delta>0
for
\alpha\in(2,5)
.
From (
10
) and a standard bootstrap argument follow a priori estimates in the same regularity range.

Secondly, analyzing the difference of solutions we show Lipschitz continuous dependence of the solutions in a space
\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
(with a low frequency weight) depending on solutions of higher regularity
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
. The corresponding short-time spaces are denoted by
\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)
,
\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)
, and
\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}&\lesssim\|v\|_{\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}+\|\partial_{x}(v(u_{1}+u_{2}))\|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)},\\
\|\partial_{x}(v(u_{1}+u_{2}))\|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}&\lesssim\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s}(T)}),\\
\|v\|^{2}_{\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}&\lesssim\|v(0)\|_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}^{2}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s}(T)}).\end{array}\right.

Lipschitz dependence of solutions in
H^{s,0}
in the
H^{-\frac{1}{2},0}
-topology follows from the above. The proof of continuous dependence in
H^{s,0}
is then concluded using the Bona–Smith argument.

Our choice of frequency dependent time localization is given by interpolation between
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
for
\alpha=2
and
T=T(N)=1
for
\alpha=5
. In the following we denote the linear propagation by

\widehat{S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}}(\xi,\eta)=e^{it(|\xi|^{\alpha}\xi-\frac{\eta^{2}}{\xi})}\hat{u}_{0}(\xi,\eta).

For
\alpha=2
on the time-scale
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
the bilinear Strichartz estimate ameliorates the derivative loss in the resonant interaction: Suppose that
N\sim N_{1}\gg N_{2}
. Hölder’s inequality gives

\begin{split}&\quad\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(S_{2}(t)P_{N_{1}}u_{0}S_{2}(t)P_{N_{2}}u_{0})\|_{L_{t}^{1}([0,T],L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}\|S(t)P_{N_{1}}u_{0}S(t)P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{t}([0,T],L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}.\end{split}

We will show the short-time bilinear Strichartz estimate in the resonant case for
T\gtrsim N^{-1}
:

\|S_{2}(t)P_{N_{1}}u_{0}S_{2}(t)P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{t}([0,T],L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}\lesssim(TN_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|P_{N_{1}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\|P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}},

which is based on showing the estimate in the above display for
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
.

Taking the above estimates together we find

\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(S_{2}(t)P_{N_{1}}u_{0}S_{2}(t)P_{N_{2}}u_{0})\|_{L_{t}^{1}([0,T],L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}\lesssim TN_{1}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|P_{N_{1}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\|P_{N_{2}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}.

This shows that indeed for
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
the nonlinear derivative interaction can be controlled for the KP-I equation. We have already indicated above how
\alpha=5
can appear as threshold for semilinear local well-posedness, explaining the choice
T=T(N)=N^{0}
.

Further remarks and thrust of the analysis.

It can be expected that the presently proved multilinear estimates yield new local well-posedness results for the original KP-I equation on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
since it appears that due to certain logarithmic divergences, the function spaces require modifications like in Robert’s work
[
32
]
. For this reason the analysis is presently not detailed.

Secondly, it is now straight-forward to prove global well-posedness for the dispersion-generalized KP-I equations posed on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma}
in the first energy space
E^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u+\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}^{2}u&=u\partial_{x}u,\quad(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma},\\
u(0)&=u_{0}\in E^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma}).\end{array}\right.

The reason is that with the frequency-dependent time localization
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
we recover the estimates proved by Zhang
[
36
]
, and for
\alpha>2
the energy space becomes smaller than for
\alpha=2
. This will allow to cover the energy space with quasilinear local well-posedness. Global well-posedness is a consequence of energy conservation.

Outline of the paper.

In Section
2
we introduce notations and function spaces to solve the nonlinear equation (
1
).
In Section
3
we show linear Strichartz estimates using
\ell^{2}
-decoupling, and in Section
4
we analyze the interplay between resonance and transversality. In the resonant case we show bilinear Strichartz estimates and show a trilinear estimate based on the nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequality. Here we moreover compare the nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequalities on product spaces obtained for dispersion-generalized KP-I equations.
In Section
5
we show that the data-to-solution mapping for (
1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
cannot be
C^{2}
for
\alpha<\frac{5}{2}
. On
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
the mapping cannot be
C^{2}
for
\alpha<5
and on tori, it cannot be expected to be
C^{2}
for any
\alpha
.
In Section
6
we show the new local well-posedness result for the KP-I equation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
based on frequency-dependent time localization, (multi)linear Strichartz estimates and the nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequality. In Section
7
we show the new well-posedness results for dispersion-generalized KP-I equations on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and in Section
8
the corresponding results on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
. Sharp results for the semilinear KP-I equations on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
are proved in Section
9
.

Basic notations:

•

Time and space variables are denoted by
(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{D}\in\{\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T}^{2}\}
. The dual variables are denoted by
(\tau,\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*}
.

•

Capital letters
M,N,L,\ldots
denote dyadic numbers in

2^{\mathbb{Z}}:=\{\ldots,1/4,1/2,1,2,4,8,\ldots\}
.

•

We write
N_{+}=\max(1,N)
. We also denote
a\vee b:=\max(a,b)
and
a\wedge b:=\min(a,b)
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.
Notations and Function spaces

2.1.
Fourier transform preliminaries

The (spatial) Fourier transform of
f:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C}
is denoted by

\hat{f}(\xi,\eta)=(\mathcal{F}_{x,y}f)(\xi,\eta)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}e^{-i(\xi,\eta)\cdot(x,y)}f(x,y)dxdy.

The inverse Fourier transform of
g:\mathbb{D}^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
is given by

f(x,y)=(\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{x,y}g)(x,y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{*}}e^{i(\xi,\eta)\cdot(x,y)}g(\xi,\eta)d\xi d\eta.

The space-time Fourier transform of
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C}
is obtained from extending the definition to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}
. We abuse notation and denote it as well as
\hat{u}
. In case
\mathbb{D}^{*}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
we indicate the counting measure by writing
(d\eta)_{1}
.

2.2.
Function spaces

Next, we introduce notations and function spaces for the nonlinear analysis to solve (
1
). Recall that the dispersion relation is given by
\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)=\xi|\xi|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{\xi}
. Let
\zeta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
denote a smooth cutoff, which is radially decreasing and satisfies
\zeta(\tau)=1
for
0\leqslant|\tau|\leqslant 1
, and
\zeta(\tau)=0
for
|\tau|\geqslant 2
. We denote
\zeta_{1}=\zeta
and for
L\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
let

\zeta_{L}(\tau)=\zeta(\tau/L)-\zeta(\tau/(L/2))
. For
N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
let
A_{N}=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:N/4\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant 4N\}
denote the
N
-annulus on the real line. We let
A_{\leqslant 1}=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:|\xi|\leqslant 2\}
. We shall write
\tilde{A}_{N}=\mathbb{R}\times A_{N}\times\mathbb{K}^{*}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
and similarly for
\tilde{A}_{\leqslant 1}
. We define

D_{\alpha,N,L}:=\{(\tau,\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*}:\xi\in A_{N},|\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)|\leqslant L\}.

We shall often omit the subscript
\alpha
to lighten the notation.

For functions
f:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
with
\text{supp}(f)\subseteq\tilde{A}_{N}
and
N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
we define:

\|f\|_{X_{N}}=\sum_{L\geqslant 1}L^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\eta_{L}(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi))f\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.

We choose the frequency-dependent time localization depending on the domains:

•

On
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we choose

T=T(N)=N^{-(5-2\alpha)-\varepsilon(\alpha)}.

This interpolates between
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
for the KP-I equation and semilinear local well-posedness for
\alpha=\frac{5}{2}
, which corresponds to
T=T(N)=N^{0}
.

•

On
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
we choose similarly

T=T(N)=N^{-\frac{5-\alpha}{3}-\varepsilon(\alpha)}.

Since in this case the evolution becomes semilinear for
\alpha\geqslant 5
, the time localization is chosen slightly stronger accordingly.

Currently, we cannot abandon the additional frequency dependent time localization for
\alpha>2
to cover some endpoint cases in the estimates. To ease notation, we write in the following
\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon(\alpha)
for brevity.

Let
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C}
with
\text{supp}(\hat{u})\subseteq\tilde{A}_{N}
. Let
N_{+}:=\max(N,1)=:N\vee 1
. We define for
N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
:

\begin{split}\|u\|_{F_{N}}&=\sup_{t_{N}\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}(\chi(N_{+}^{\frac{5-\alpha}{3}+\varepsilon}(t-t_{N}))u)\|_{X_{N}},\\
\|u\|_{\mathcal{N}_{N}}&=\sup_{t_{N}\in\mathbb{R}}\|(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)+iN_{+}^{\frac{5-\alpha}{3}+\varepsilon})^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}(\chi(N_{+}^{\frac{5-\alpha}{3}+\varepsilon}(t-t_{N}))u)\|_{X_{N}}.\end{split}

These function spaces can be localized for
T\in(0,1]
:

E_{N}=\{f:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\hat{f}\text{ supported in }A_{N},\;\|f\|_{E_{N}}=\|f\|_{L^{2}}\}

Let
u\in C([0,T],E_{N})
. Then we define

\|u\|_{F_{N}(T)}=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{u}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C},\\
\tilde{u}\big|_{[0,T]}=u\end{subarray}}\|\tilde{u}\|_{F_{N}},\quad\|u\|_{\mathcal{N}_{N}(T)}=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{u}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C},\\
\tilde{u}\big|_{[0,T]}=u\end{subarray}}\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{N}_{N}}.

We assemble the function spaces by Littlewood-Paley decomposition. Let
u\in C([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{D}))
. Then

\|u\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)}=\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}}N_{+}^{2s}\|P_{N}u\|_{F_{N}(T)}^{2},\quad\|u\|^{2}_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)}=\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}}N_{+}^{2s}\|P_{N}u\|^{2}_{\mathcal{N}_{N}(T)}.

Moreover, we define

\|u\|_{B^{s}(T)}^{2}=\sum_{N\geqslant 1}N^{2s}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|P_{N}u(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|P_{\leqslant 1}u(0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

The following function space properties are standard (see e.g.
[
15
]
) and will be used in the following. For
K,L\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, and
f_{N}\in X_{N}
, the following estimate holds:

\begin{split}&\sum_{J\geqslant L}J^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|\zeta_{J}(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta))\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|f_{N}(\tau,\xi,\eta)\big|L^{-1}(1+L^{-1}|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{-4})d\tau^{\prime}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\quad+L^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|\zeta_{\leqslant L}(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta))\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f_{N}(\tau^{\prime},\xi,\eta)|L^{-1}(1+L^{-1}|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|)^{-4}d\tau^{\prime}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim\|f_{N}\|_{X_{N}}\end{split}

with implicit constant independent of
J
and
L
.

For
\gamma\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and
K,L\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, and
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, it follows

\|\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[\gamma(L(t-t_{0}))\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}(f_{k})]\|_{X_{K}}\lesssim\|f_{K}\|_{X_{K}}.

The implicit constant is independent of
K
,
L
, and
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Next, we introduce admissible time multiplication. Let
N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and define the set
S_{N}
of
N
-acceptable time multiplication factors as

S_{N}=\{m_{N}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\,:\,\|m_{N}\|_{S_{N}}=\sum_{0\leqslant j\leqslant 30}N_{+}^{-(\frac{5-\alpha}{3}+\varepsilon)j}\|\partial^{j}m_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty\}.

The following estimates hold for any
s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
and
T\in(0,1]
(cf.
[
21
, p. 273]
):

(11)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\|\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(m_{N}(t)P_{N}u)\|_{F^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\big(\sup\limits_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\|m_{N}\|_{S_{N}}\big)\|u\|_{F^{s}(T)},\\
\|\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(m_{N}(t)P_{N}u)\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\big(\sup\limits_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\|m_{N}\|_{S_{N}}\big)\|u\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)},\\
\|\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}(m_{N}(t)P_{N}u)\|_{E^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\big(\sup\limits_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}\|m_{N}\|_{S_{N}}\big)\|u\|_{E^{s}(T)}.\end{array}\right.

We recall the embedding
F^{s}(T)\hookrightarrow C([-T,T];H^{s,0})
for short-time
X^{s,b}
-spaces.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
. For all
T\in(0,1]
and
u\in F^{s}(T)
we have

(12)

\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}\|u(t)\|_{H^{s,0}}\lesssim\|u\|_{F^{s}(T)}.

For variants of the lemma on different domains we cite
[
21
,
19
]
.

The following plays the role of an energy estimate in short-time Fourier restriction spaces (cf.
[
34
, Proposition 2.5.2]
):

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
. For all
T\in(0,1]
and (mild) solutions

u\in C([-T,T],E^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}))
of

\partial_{t}u-\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u-\partial_{x}^{-1}\Delta_{y}u=\partial_{x}(u^{2})\text{ in }\mathbb{D}_{\lambda}\times(-T,T),

the following estimate holds:

\|u\|_{F^{s}(T)}\lesssim\|u\|_{E^{s}(T)}+\|\partial_{x}(u^{2})\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)}.

For the large data result we recall how to trade regularity in the modulation variable for powers of the time localization. For
b\in\mathbb{R}
define

\|f_{N}\|_{X^{b}_{N}}=\sum_{L\geq 1}\|\eta_{L}(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta))f_{N}(\tau,\xi,\eta)\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}

and the short-time function spaces
F_{N}^{b}
,
\mathcal{N}_{N}^{b}
, which are defined following along the above, but based on
X^{b}_{N}
.

We have the following:

Lemma 2.3
.

[
15
, Lemma 3.4]

Let
T\in(0,1]
, and
b<\frac{1}{2}
. Then it holds:

\|P_{N}u\|_{F_{N}^{b}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}-b-}\|P_{N}u\|_{F_{N}}

for any function
u:[-T,T]\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C}
.

3.
Linear Strichartz estimates

3.1.
Linear Strichartz estimates on Euclidean space

Hadac
[
17
]
proved linear Strichartz estimates for generalized KP-II equations in
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
. The proof extends to KP-I equations, which leads to the following:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\alpha\geqslant 2
,
2<q\leqslant\infty
,
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
, and
s=(1-\frac{2}{r})(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{4})
. Then we have

\|S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|_{L_{t}^{q}(\mathbb{R};L_{xy}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

For
q=r=4
we obtain the estimate

\|S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|_{L_{t}^{4}(\mathbb{R};L_{xy}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{\gamma,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

with
\gamma=\frac{2-\alpha}{8}
.

3.2.
Linear Strichartz estimates on cylinders

In the following we use
\ell^{2}
-decoupling due to Bourgain–Demeter
[
9
]
for elliptic hypersurfaces to show linear Strichartz estimates on cylinders.
We state the special case of decoupling in
2+1
-dimensions for convenience:

Theorem 3.2

(
\ell^{2}
-decoupling for elliptic hypersurfaces)
.

Let
\Lambda>0
,
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact, and
\varphi:A\to\mathbb{R}
be a
C^{2}
-function such that

S=\{(\xi,\varphi(\xi)):\,\xi\in A\}

has principal curvatures in
[\Lambda^{-1},\Lambda]
.
Define the Fourier extension operator adapted to
S
by

\mathcal{E}_{S}f(x)=\int_{A}e^{i(x^{\prime},x_{3})\cdot(\xi,\varphi(\xi))}f(\xi)d\xi,\quad x=(x^{\prime},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}.

Let
R\geqslant 1
. The following estimate holds:

(13)

\|\mathcal{E}_{S}f\|_{L^{4}_{x}(B(0,R))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}R^{\varepsilon}\big(\sum_{\theta:R^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\text{ball}}\|\mathcal{E}_{S}f_{\theta}\|_{L^{4}(w_{B(0,R)})}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the sum ranges over a finitely overlapping collection of balls
\theta
of radius
R^{-\frac{1}{2}}
covering
A
. The implicit constant in (
13
) depends on
S
,
\Lambda
, but not on
R
.

We now formulate its consequence for generalized KP-I equations:

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
A\in\mathbb{R}
, and
u_{0}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
with
\text{supp}(\hat{u}_{0})\subseteq\{(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|\xi|\sim N,\;\big|\frac{\eta}{\xi}-A\big|\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\}.
Then the following estimate holds:

\|P_{N}S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|_{L_{t}^{4}([0,1],L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\frac{2-\alpha}{8}+\varepsilon}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})}.

Proof.

First, we consider the case
|A|\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, in which case we find
|\eta|\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}
.
We use the anisotropic scaling

(14)

x\to Nx,\quad y\to N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}y,\quad t\to N^{\alpha+1}t,\qquad\xi\to\frac{\xi}{N},\quad\eta\to\frac{\eta}{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}

to find

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\|P_{N}S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|^{4}_{L^{4}_{t,x,y}([0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}=N^{-(\alpha+1)}N^{-4(1-\frac{1}{4})}N^{-(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Big\|\int_{*}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0}(N\xi^{\prime},N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\eta^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\Big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime}}([0,N^{\alpha+1}],L^{4}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/(2\pi N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\mathbb{Z}))}^{4}.\end{split}

Note that in the rescaled support of
\hat{u}_{0}
it holds
|\xi^{\prime}|\sim 1
and
|\eta^{\prime}|\lesssim 1
.

Now we enlarge the
y
-domain of integration
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\mathbb{Z})
perceived as an interval in
\mathbb{R}
to an interval of size
N^{\alpha+1}
. By spatial periodicity this amounts to a factor of
(N^{\alpha+1}/N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1})^{-1}=N^{-\alpha/2}
, and we continue the above as

(15)

\begin{split}&\;N^{-(\alpha+1)}N^{-3}N^{-\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1\big)}N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\\
&\times\Big\|\int_{*}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}(|\xi^{\prime}|^{\alpha}\xi^{\prime}+(\eta^{\prime})^{2}/\xi^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0}(N\xi^{\prime},N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\eta^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\Big\|^{4}_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime}}([0,N^{\alpha+1}],L^{4}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,N^{\alpha+1}]))}.\end{split}

We invoke
\ell^{2}
-decoupling for elliptic surfaces in two dimensions. Here we note that the Hessian is for
\xi^{\prime}\neq 0
given by

\partial^{2}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha(\alpha+1)\text{sgn}(\xi^{\prime})|\xi^{\prime}|^{\alpha-1}+2\frac{(\eta^{\prime})^{2}}{(\xi^{\prime})^{3}}&-\frac{2\eta^{\prime}}{(\xi^{\prime})^{2}}\\
-\frac{2\eta^{\prime}}{(\xi^{\prime})^{2}}&\frac{2}{\xi^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

It is easy to see that
\partial^{2}\omega_{\alpha}
is uniformly elliptic for
|\xi^{\prime}|\sim 1
and
|\eta^{\prime}|\lesssim 1
. Indeed, we compute

\det\partial^{2}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=\alpha(\alpha-1)|\xi^{\prime}|^{\alpha-2},\quad|\text{tr}(\partial^{2}\omega_{\alpha})|\sim 1.

We infer for
\xi^{\prime}\sim 1
two positive eigenvalues of size comparable to
1
, and for
\xi^{\prime}\sim-1
two negative eigenvalues of size comparable to
-1
. The latter case can be reduced to the elliptic case by time reversal.

Few remarks about decoupling are in order: To apply decoupling as formulated in Theorem
3.2
, we break the domain of integration
[0,N^{\alpha+1}]\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,N^{\alpha+1}]
into finitely overlapping balls of size
N^{\alpha+1}
. The shift in
x
is admissible by translation invariance.
Secondly, decoupling requires a continuous approximation, i.e., approximating the exponential sum with a Fourier extension operator by mollifying the Dirac comb.

We give the details: Firstly, we break the
x
-integration into balls of size
N^{\alpha+1}
.

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\big\|\int e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0}(N\xi^{\prime},N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\eta^{\prime})d\xi(d\eta^{\prime})_{N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}([0,N^{\alpha+1}]\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,N^{\alpha+1}])}^{4}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{B_{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}}\big\|\int e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0N}d\xi(d\eta^{\prime})_{N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}([0,N^{\alpha+1}]\times B_{N^{\alpha+1}}\times[0,N^{\alpha+1}])}^{4}.\end{split}

Next, by a linear change of variables
x^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}+c(B_{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}})
with
c(B)
denoting the center of the ball, we can suppose that the ball is centered at the origin. The phase factor is absorbed into
\hat{u}_{0N}
.

For the continuous approximation, we consider a sequence of smooth functions
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
such that for
|x^{\prime}|,|t^{\prime}|,|y^{\prime}|\lesssim N^{\alpha+1}
we have

\int e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{f}_{\lambda}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\to\int e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0N}d\xi^{\prime}(d\eta^{\prime})_{N^{-(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1)}}.

More specifically, we can choose
\text{supp}(\hat{f}_{\lambda})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\lambda^{-1}}(\text{supp}(\hat{u}_{0N}))
in a
\lambda^{-1}
-neighbourhood. Then, choosing
\lambda
large enough, we can estimate by the theorem of dominated convergence:

\begin{split}&\quad\big\|\int e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0N}d\xi(d\eta^{\prime})_{N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}([0,N^{\alpha+1}]\times B_{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}\times\mathbb{T}_{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}})}\\
&\lesssim\big\|\int e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{f}_{\lambda}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}(B_{N^{\alpha+1}})}.\end{split}

The final expression is amenable to decoupling as recorded and after reversing the continuous approximation by letting
\lambda\to\infty
, we obtain an
\ell^{2}
-sum into balls on the frequency side of size
N^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}
. On this scale the phase function can be trivialized by Taylor expansion and we obtain from integration in time after reversing the continuous approximation:

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\big\|\int_{*}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}(|\xi^{\prime}|^{\alpha}\xi^{\prime}+(\eta^{\prime})^{2}/\xi^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0\theta}(N\xi^{\prime},N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\eta^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\big\|^{2}_{L_{t^{\prime}}^{4}([0,N^{\alpha+1}],L^{4}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(B_{N^{\alpha+1}})}\\
&\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}\big\|\int_{*}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime})}\hat{u}_{0\theta}(N\xi^{\prime},N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\eta^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\big\|^{2}_{L^{4}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(B_{{N^{\alpha+1}}})}.\end{split}

Now we reverse the scaling in
x
and
y
, which compensates the scaling factors
N^{-3}
and
N^{-(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1)}
in (
15
).

It remains to estimate

\big\|\int_{*}e^{i(x\xi+y\eta)}\hat{u}_{0\theta}(\xi,\eta)d\xi d\eta\big\|^{2}_{L^{4}_{xy}}

with
\theta
denoting a rectangle of size
N^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}\times N^{\frac{1}{2}}
. We can use Bernstein’s inequality to find

(16)

\big\|\int_{*}e^{i(x\xi+y\eta)}\hat{u}_{0\theta}(\xi,\eta)d\xi d\eta\big\|_{L^{4}_{xy}}\lesssim N^{\frac{1-\alpha}{8}}N^{\frac{1}{8}}\|u_{0\theta}\|_{L^{2}_{xy}}.

We summarize the estimates as follows: Scaling to unit frequencies and enlarging the spatial domain to
N^{\alpha+1}
incurs a factor of

N^{-(\alpha+1)}N^{-3}N^{-(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1)}N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

Invoking
\ell^{2}
-decoupling incurs a factor of
N^{\varepsilon}
and decouples the integral into balls of size
N^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}
. Carrying out the time integral yields a factor
N^{\alpha+1}
and reversing the scaling in
x
and
\xi
, and
y
and
\eta
gives factors
N^{3}
and
N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}
, respectively. Moreover, we use periodicity to decrease the spatial domain to
\mathbb{T}
again after inverting the scaling. This incurs a factor
N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.

We have proved so far

(17)

\|P_{N}S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|^{4}_{L_{t,x,y}^{4}([0,1],\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\varepsilon}\big(\sum_{\theta}\|u_{0\theta}\|^{2}_{L^{4}_{xy}}\big)^{2}

with the sum over
\theta
being over essentially disjoint rectangles of size
N^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}\times N^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Plugging (
16
) into (
17
) yields

\|P_{N}S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|^{4}_{L^{4}_{t,x,y}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\varepsilon+\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{xy}}^{4},

which completes the proof in case
|A|\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.

Now we turn to the case
|A|\gg N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
: In the first step we apply the scaling (
14
), which maps the Fourier support to the set

|\xi^{\prime}|\sim 1,\quad\big|\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{\xi^{\prime}}-A^{\prime}\big|\lesssim 1

for
A^{\prime}=A/N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
. Like above, by periodic extension in
y^{\prime}
, we reduce to estimate the expression

\big\|\int e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0}(N\xi^{\prime},N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\eta^{\prime})d\xi^{\prime}(d\eta^{\prime})_{N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime}}([0,N^{\alpha+1}],L^{4}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}\times B_{N^{\alpha+1}}))}.

We can apply the Galilean invariance

(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})=F^{\prime}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}-A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime})

to obtain

|\xi^{\prime\prime}|\sim 1,\quad\big|\frac{\eta^{\prime\prime}}{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\big|\lesssim 1.

Note that this does not change the domain of integration
\mathbb{R}\times B_{N^{\alpha+1}}
, but it is important to note that in general
(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\notin\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
. Now we again break the domain of integration
[0,N^{\alpha+1}]\times\mathbb{R}\times B_{N^{\alpha+1}}
into balls of size
B_{N^{\alpha+1}}
and use translation invariance.

We have by continuous approximation

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\big\|\int d\xi^{\prime\prime}\sum_{\eta^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}-A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}))}\hat{u}_{0N}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}+A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime})\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime}}([0,N^{\alpha+1}],L^{4}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(B_{N^{\alpha+1}}))}\\
&\lesssim\big\|\int_{|\xi^{\prime\prime}|\sim 1,\,|\eta^{\prime\prime}|\lesssim 1}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}))}\hat{f}_{\lambda}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})d\xi^{\prime\prime}d\eta^{\prime\prime}\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}(B_{N^{\alpha+1}})}.\end{split}

Now we can apply
\ell^{2}
-decoupling and reverse the continuous approximation, which gives the estimate

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\big\|\int d\xi^{\prime\prime}\sum_{\eta^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}-A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})}\hat{u}_{0,N}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}+A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime})\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}(B_{N^{\alpha+1}})}\\
&\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\varepsilon}\big(\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{B}_{N^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}}}\big\|\int d\xi^{\prime\prime}\sum_{\eta^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}-A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}}e^{i(x^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime}\eta^{\prime\prime}+t^{\prime}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}))}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\times\chi_{\theta}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\hat{u}_{0N}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}+A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime})\big\|_{L^{4}_{t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}(w_{B_{N^{\alpha+1}}})}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

The sum over
\theta
is over essentially disjoint
N^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}
-balls, which cover the set
\{|\xi^{\prime\prime}|\sim 1,|\eta^{\prime\prime}|\lesssim 1\}
.

Summing over the
B_{N^{\alpha+1}}
-balls in the
x
-coordinate and reversing all linear transformations and the scalings, we arrive at the expression

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\big\|\int d\xi\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i(x\xi+y\eta+t\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta))}\hat{u}_{0}(\xi,\eta)\big\|_{L_{t}^{4}([0,1],L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}\\
&\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\varepsilon}\big(\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{B}_{N^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}}}\big\|\int d\xi\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i(x\xi+y\eta+t\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta))}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\times\chi_{\theta}(N^{-1}\xi,N^{-(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1)}(\eta-A\xi))\hat{u}_{0}(\xi,\eta)\big\|^{2}_{L_{t}^{4}(w_{1},L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))}.\end{split}

Since there are no oscillations of
\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)
for
(N^{-1}\xi,N^{-(\frac{\alpha}{2}+1)}(\eta-A\xi))
contained in
\theta
, we can carry out the integration in time without loss.

Let
\bar{\theta}=I_{N^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}}\times I_{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}
be the anisotropic dilation of
\theta
. Let

(\bar{\xi},\bar{\eta})=F(\xi,\eta)=(\xi,\eta-A\xi)

denote the Galilean transform without anisotropic dilation.

To conclude the argument, we apply Bernstein’s inequality for which we need to understand
F^{-1}(\bar{\theta})
. Since
\xi\in I_{N^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}}
the condition
\eta-A\xi\in I_{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}
yields the bound

\#\{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}\;|\;\exists\xi\in I_{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}:\eta-A\xi\in I_{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\}\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}+AN^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}\sim AN^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}.

The final estimate is due to the size assumption on
A
. For fixed
\eta
, we estimate the measure of
\xi
such that
F(\xi,\eta)\in\bar{\theta}
as

\text{meas}(\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:F(\xi,\eta)\in\bar{\theta}\})\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}/A.

Then it is a consequence of Bernstein’s inequality that

\begin{split}&\quad\big\|\int_{F(\xi,\eta)\in\bar{\theta}}e^{i(x\xi+y\eta)}\hat{f}(\xi,\eta)d\xi d\eta\big\|_{L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})}\\
&\lesssim(AN^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}})^{\frac{1}{4}}(N^{\frac{1}{2}}/A)^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\int_{(\xi,\eta)\in F^{-1}(\bar{\theta})}e^{i(x\xi+y\eta)}\hat{u}_{0}(\xi,\eta)\|_{L^{2}_{xy}}\\
&\lesssim N^{\frac{2-\alpha}{8}}\|\int_{(\xi,\eta)\in F^{-1}(\bar{\theta})}e^{i(x\xi+y\eta)}\hat{u}_{0}(\xi,\eta)\|_{L^{2}_{xy}}.\end{split}

The proof is then concluded by the essential disjointness of
F^{-1}(\bar{\theta})
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

We remark that the argument does not yield an improved estimate when considering frequency-dependent time intervals
T=T(N)=N^{-\kappa}
. Moreover, a comparison of the estimate on cylinders with the Strichartz estimates on Euclidean space indicates sharpness of the derivative loss up to endpoints.

3.3.
Linear Strichartz estimates on tori

The above arguments yield the following result on tori:

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
\alpha\geqslant 2
, and
\text{supp}(\hat{u}_{0})\subseteq\{(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|\xi|\sim N,\quad|\eta|\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\}=A_{N,N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}
. Then the following estimate holds:

(18)

\|S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|_{L_{t}^{4}([0,1],L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma}))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\frac{1}{8}+\varepsilon}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})}.

Proof.

We can follow along the arguments of the proof of Proposition
3.1
, i.e., employ the anisotropic scaling (
14
), use a continuous approximation to invoke
\ell^{2}
-decoupling, and finally reverse the continuous approximation and scaling. After carrying out the integration in time, we arrive at the expression:

\|S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|_{L_{t}^{4}([0,1],L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma}))}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N^{\varepsilon}\big(\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{R}_{N^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}\times N^{\frac{1}{2}}}}\|u_{0\theta}\|^{2}_{L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the sum is carried out over essentially disjoint rectangles
\theta
of size
N^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}\times N^{\frac{1}{2}}
, which cover
A_{N,N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}
. An application of Bernstein’s inequality yields

\|u_{0\theta}\|_{L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})}\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{8}}\|u_{0\theta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})},

since presently the estimate is carried out with counting measure compared to the previous section. The proof is concluded by essential disjointness of
\theta
.
∎

Remark 3.6

(Sharpness of the Strichartz estimate)
.

The Strichartz estimate is sharp, which can be seen from considering the initial data

\hat{u}_{0}(\xi,\eta)=\delta_{\xi,N}1_{[1,N^{\frac{1}{2}}]}(\eta),\quad\delta_{N}(\xi)1_{[1,N^{\frac{1}{2}}]}(\eta).

In this case, there are no oscillations, for which reason

\|S_{\alpha}(t)u_{0}\|_{L_{t}^{4}([0,1],L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})}\sim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})}.

Next, we observe by
u_{0}(0)=N^{\frac{1}{2}}
and the uncertainty principle that

\|u_{0}\|_{L^{4}_{xy}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma})}\gtrsim N^{\frac{1}{2}}(N^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{4}}\sim N^{\frac{3}{8}}.

Since
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{xy}}\sim N^{\frac{1}{4}}
, this example exhausts (
18
) up to the endpoint.

This points out that for the KP-I dispersion relation the Strichartz estimates on tori deviate significantly from the Strichartz estimates on Euclidean space, which is not the case for the Schrödinger equation. For the Schrödinger evolution,
\ell^{2}
-decoupling recovers Euclidean Strichartz estimates on finite times up to arbitrarily small derivative loss (see
[
9
]
).

4.
Resonance, Transversality, and multilinear estimates

In this section we show bilinear Strichartz estimates and trilinear convolution estimates for approximate solutions to dispersion-generalized KP-I equations.

4.1.
Resonance and bilinear Strichartz estimates

We recall resonance and transversality identities from
[
33
]
. The resonance function is given by

\begin{split}\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})&=\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})\\
&=\underbrace{|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})-|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}\xi_{1}-|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}\xi_{2}}_{\Omega_{\alpha,1}}-\frac{(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})^{2}}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}.\end{split}

We say that we are in the
resonant case
, if

(19)

|\Omega_{\alpha}|\ll\big|\Omega_{\alpha,1}\big|.

Suppose that
|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|\sim|\xi_{1}|\sim N_{\max}\gtrsim N_{\min}\sim|\xi_{2}|
: Applying the mean-value theorem gives

|\Omega_{\alpha,1}|\sim N_{\max}^{\alpha}N_{\min}.

We see that in the non-resonant case the derivative loss is readily recovered, e.g., using standard Fourier restriction spaces (cf.
[
7
,
8
]
) because
|\Omega_{\alpha,1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\gtrsim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}_{\max}N_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

We focus on the resonant case. Here we obtain a bound for the transversality quantified by the difference of the group velocity:

\nabla\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)=((\alpha+1)|\xi|^{\alpha}-\frac{\eta^{2}}{\xi^{2}},\frac{2\eta}{\xi}).

It follows

(20)

|\partial_{\eta}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})-\partial_{\eta}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})|\gtrsim\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta_{2}}{\xi_{2}}\big|\sim N_{\max}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

This is a key ingredient for the bilinear Strichartz estimates in the resonant case.
Define

(21)

C_{1,\mathbb{D}}(L,N)=\begin{cases}(L/N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}},\;&\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\langle L/N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}},\;&\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}.\end{cases}

For later use we show the following more general bilinear Strichartz estimate:

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\mathbb{D}\in\{\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\}
. Let
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
,
i=1,2
satisfy the support conditions
\pi_{\xi}(\text{supp}(f_{j}))\subseteq I_{j}
with
|I_{j}|\lesssim N_{\min}
and

(22)

|\partial_{\eta}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})-\partial_{\eta}\omega_{\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{1},\eta-\eta_{1})|\gtrsim N_{\max}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}

for
(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\in\pi_{\xi,\eta}(\text{supp}(f_{1}))
,
(\xi-\xi_{1},\eta-\eta_{1})\in\pi_{\xi,\eta}(\text{supp}(f_{2}))
, and
|\tau_{i}-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{i},\eta_{i})|\lesssim L_{i}
for
(\tau_{i},\xi_{i},\eta_{i})\in\text{supp}(f_{i})
. Then the following estimate holds:

(23)

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim N_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{1,\mathbb{D}}(L_{\max},N_{\max})\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.

Proof.

Suppose that
L_{1}=\min(L_{1},L_{2})
and
L_{2}=\max(L_{1},L_{2})
by symmetry. We obtain by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

\begin{split}&\quad\|(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\leqslant\sup_{(\tau,\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\text{meas}(S=\{(\tau_{1},\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\in\text{supp}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}),\;\xi_{1}\in I_{1}:\\
&\quad\quad(\tau-\tau_{1},\xi-\xi_{1},\eta-\eta_{1})\in\text{supp}(f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}),\;\xi-\xi_{1}\in I_{2}\})^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L_{\tau,\xi,\eta}^{2}}.\end{split}

The assumption (
22
) yields

\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta-\eta_{1}}{\xi-\xi_{1}}\big|\gtrsim N_{\max}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

Note moreover that

(24)

\big|\big(\tau_{1}-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\big)+\big((\tau-\tau_{1})-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{1},\eta-\eta_{1})\big)\big|\lesssim L_{2}.

We estimate the measure of
S
by fixing
\xi_{1}
, which amounts to a factor
|I_{1}|
, and counting
\tau_{1}
and
\eta_{1}
. From (
22
) and (
24
) follows that for fixed
\tau
,
\xi_{1}
,
\xi
,
\eta
that on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
the measure of
\eta_{1}
is estimated by

\big(\frac{L_{2}}{N_{\max}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. On
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
there are at most
\langle\frac{L_{2}}{N_{\max}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}
values of
\eta_{1}
such that
(\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\tau_{1})\in S
. This is a consequence of the mean-value theorem. Finally, with
(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})
fixed, we trivially estimate

\text{meas}(\{\tau_{1}:|\tau_{1}-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})|\leqslant L_{1}\})\leqslant L_{1}.

In conclusion we obtain

\begin{split}\text{meas}(S)&\leqslant|I_{1}|\cdot\#\{\eta_{1}:\eqref{eq:GroupVelocityGeneral}\text{ and }\eqref{eq:RangeEta1General}\text{ holds }\}\cdot\big|\{\tau_{1}:|\tau_{1}-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\big|\leqslant L_{1}\}\big|\\
&\lesssim N_{\min}\cdot C_{1,\mathbb{D}}(L_{2},N_{\max})\cdot L_{1}.\end{split}

with
C_{1,\mathbb{D}}
defined in (
21
). This finishes the proof.
∎

We shall see that the support assumptions are satisfied in the resonant case, which implies:

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
\mathbb{D}\in\{\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\}
,
N_{3}\sim N_{1}\gtrsim N_{2}
and for
i=1,2
:
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{N_{i},L_{i}}
with
L_{i}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
for
i=1,2,3
. Then the following estimate holds with
C_{1,\mathbb{D}}
defined in (
21
):

(25)

\begin{split}&\quad\|1_{D_{\alpha,N_{3},L_{3}}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*})}\\
&\lesssim(L_{1}\wedge L_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{1,\mathbb{D}}(L_{1}\vee L_{2},N_{\max})\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*})}.\end{split}

Proof.

Since

\begin{split}&\quad|\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\xi-\xi_{1},\eta-\eta_{1})|\\
&=|\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)-(\tau_{1}-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1}))-((\tau-\tau_{1})-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{1},\eta-\eta_{1}))|\\
&\ll N_{\max}^{\alpha}N_{\min},\end{split}

we are in the resonant case (
19
) and we have by (
20
)

(26)

\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta-\eta_{1}}{\xi-\xi_{1}}\big|\sim N_{\max}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

The assumptions of Proposition
4.1
are satisfied, which yields the claim.
∎

We have the following alternative bilinear Strichartz estimate, which is based on the second order transversality:

|\partial^{2}_{\eta}(\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})+\omega_{\alpha}(\xi-\xi_{1},\eta-\eta_{1})|=2\Big|\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}+\frac{1}{\xi-\xi_{1}}\Big|.

The estimate was observed by Bourgain
[
8
]
in the context of the KP-II equation. This will serve to estimate several boundary cases, in case the low frequency is very small or the resonance is very large. Define

(27)

C_{2,\mathbb{D}}(L,N)=\begin{cases}(LN)^{\frac{1}{4}},\quad&\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\langle LN\rangle^{\frac{1}{4}},\quad&\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}.\end{cases}

Lemma 4.3

(
[
8
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{D}\in\{\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\}
, and
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
with
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{N_{i},L_{i}}
and
\pi_{\xi}(\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}))\subseteq I_{i}
with
|I_{i}|\leqslant K
. Then the following estimate holds:

(28)

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim K^{\frac{1}{2}}(L_{1}\wedge L_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{2,\mathbb{D}}(L_{1}\vee L_{2},N_{\min})\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}.

We have the following consequence of the above bilinear Strichartz estimates and the Córdoba–Fefferman square function estimate:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\alpha\geqslant 1
,
N_{1}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
N_{2}\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
N_{1}\gg N_{2}
,
L_{1},L_{2}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. For
i=1,2
, let
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
with

\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{N_{i},L_{i}}
.

Then the following estimate holds:

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim\frac{N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{4}}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

If
L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
, the estimate is a consequence of Lemma
4.3
:

\begin{split}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}&\lesssim N_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}}L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{\max}^{\frac{1}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}\\
&\lesssim\frac{N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{4}}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

In the following we suppose that
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
. We carry out a dyadic decomposition (Whitney) in the transversality parameter:

D\sim\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta_{2}}{\xi_{2}}\big|

with the range
D\in[\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\infty)
:

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\leqslant\sum_{D\geqslant\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{I_{1}^{D}\sim I_{2}^{D}}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}^{I_{1}^{D}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}^{I_{2}^{D}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.

Here we have broken the support of
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
into intervals
I_{i}^{D}
of
(\eta_{i}/\xi_{i})
of length
\sim D
with

\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta_{2}}{\xi_{2}}\big|\sim D\text{ for }\frac{\eta_{i}}{\xi_{i}}\in I_{i}^{D}.

The contribution of
D\gtrsim N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
is handled by Proposition
4.1
:

\begin{split}\sum_{D\gtrsim N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{I_{1}^{D}\sim I_{2}^{D}}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}^{I_{1}^{D}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}^{I_{2}^{D}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}&\lesssim\sum_{D\gtrsim N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{I_{1}^{D}\sim I_{2}^{D}}\frac{N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{D^{\frac{1}{2}}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}^{I_{i}^{D}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim\frac{N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{4}}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

For
D\ll N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
we can use an almost orthogonal decomposition to effectively reduce the
\xi
-support of
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
. Without loss of generality we can suppose that
\pi_{\xi}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{>0}
by complex conjugation and symmetry of the dispersion relation.

The convolution constraint reads

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}&=\xi_{3}+\xi_{4},\\
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}&=\eta_{3}+\eta_{4},\\
\xi_{1}^{\alpha+1}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{\xi_{1}}+\xi_{2}^{\alpha+1}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}}&=\xi_{3}^{\alpha+1}+\frac{\eta_{3}^{2}}{\xi_{3}}+\xi_{4}^{\alpha+1}+\frac{\eta_{4}^{2}}{\xi_{4}}+\mathcal{O}(L_{\max}).\end{array}\right.

We rescale to unit
\xi
-frequencies by
\xi\to\frac{\xi}{N_{1}}
,
\eta\to\frac{\eta}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}
to find the following for the renormalized frequencies:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\xi^{\prime}_{1}+\xi^{\prime}_{2}&=\xi^{\prime}_{3}+\xi^{\prime}_{4},\\
\eta^{\prime}_{1}+\eta^{\prime}_{2}&=\eta^{\prime}_{3}+\eta^{\prime}_{4},\\
(\xi^{\prime}_{1})^{\alpha+1}+\frac{(\eta^{\prime}_{1})^{2}}{\xi^{\prime}_{1}}+(\xi^{\prime}_{2})^{\alpha+1}+\frac{(\eta^{\prime}_{2})^{2}}{\xi^{\prime}_{2}}&=(\xi^{\prime}_{3})^{\alpha+1}+\frac{(\eta^{\prime}_{3})^{2}}{\xi^{\prime}_{3}}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt+(\xi^{\prime}_{4})^{\alpha+1}+\frac{(\eta^{\prime}_{4})^{2}}{\xi^{\prime}_{4}}+\mathcal{O}(L_{\max}/N_{1}^{\alpha+1}).\end{array}\right.

We subtract

\frac{(\eta_{1}^{\prime}+\eta_{2}^{\prime})^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{(\eta_{3}^{\prime}+\eta_{4}^{\prime})^{2}}{\xi_{3}^{\prime}+\xi_{4}^{\prime}}

from the third equation to find

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime}&=\xi_{3}^{\prime}+\xi_{4}^{\prime},\\
(\xi_{1}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}+(\xi_{2}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}-\frac{(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\eta_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime})}&=(\xi_{3}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}+(\xi_{4}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}\\
&\quad\quad-\frac{(\eta_{3}^{\prime}\xi_{4}^{\prime}-\eta_{4}^{\prime}\xi_{3}^{\prime})^{2}}{\xi_{3}^{\prime}\xi_{4}^{\prime}(\xi_{3}^{\prime}+\xi_{4}^{\prime})}+\mathcal{O}(L_{\max}/N_{1}^{\alpha+1}).\end{array}\right.

This yields

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime}&=\xi_{3}^{\prime}+\xi_{4}^{\prime},\\
(\xi_{1}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}+(\xi_{2}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}&=(\xi_{3}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}+(\xi_{4}^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{D^{2}N_{2}}{N_{1}^{\alpha+1}}+\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{1}^{\alpha+1}}).\end{array}\right.

Note that for our Whitney decomposition we always have
D^{2}N_{2}\gtrsim L_{\max}
.
Note that the curve
\xi^{\prime}\mapsto(\xi^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}
degenerates at the origin for
\alpha>-1
. To still find an almost orthogonality resembling the Córdoba–Fefferman square function estimate, we rewrite the second line as

f^{\prime}(\xi_{1*})(\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{3}^{\prime})+f^{\prime}(\xi_{2*})(\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{4}^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{D^{2}N_{2}}{N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{1}}\big).

We have
f^{\prime}(\xi_{2*})=f^{\prime}(\xi_{1*})+\Delta
with
|\Delta|\gtrsim 1
, for which reason

f^{\prime}(\xi_{1*})(\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{3}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{4}^{\prime})+\Delta(\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\xi_{4}^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{D^{2}N_{2}}{N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{1}}\big).

Consequently,
\xi_{i}^{\prime}=\xi_{i+2}^{\prime}+\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{D^{2}N_{2}}{N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{1}}\big)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and we obtain an almost orthogonal decomposition of

\|f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim\sum_{I_{1}^{\prime},I^{\prime}_{2}}\|f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1},I^{\prime}_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2},I^{\prime}_{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

with
\xi^{\prime}
-intervals of length
\mathcal{O}(\frac{D^{2}N_{2}}{N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}})
.
After rescaling we obtain a decomposition into intervals of length
D^{2}N_{2}/N_{1}^{\alpha}
.

First we handle the contribution
D\gg L_{\max}/N_{2}
. Applying the bilinear Strichartz estimate Proposition
4.1
gives

\|f^{I_{1}}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{I_{2}}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim\frac{DN_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}D^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f^{I_{i}}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.

Summation in the intervals can be carried out by almost orthogonality. Summation in
L_{\max}/N_{2}\ll D\lesssim N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
gives

\sum_{L_{\max}/N_{2}\ll D\lesssim N_{1}^{\alpha/2}}\frac{D^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\lesssim\frac{N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{4}}}.

For the contribution
D\sim\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
we use Lemma
4.3
after almost orthgonal decomposition into intervals of length
\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{1}^{\alpha}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
:

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}^{I_{1}}*f^{I_{2}}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{1}^{\alpha}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{4}}L_{\max}^{\frac{1}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}^{I_{i}}\|_{2}.

By the upper bound for
L_{\max}
this gives

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}^{I_{1}}*f^{I_{2}}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim\frac{N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{4}}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}^{I_{i}}\|_{2}.

This handles all possible transversalities
D\in[\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},N_{1}^{\alpha}]
. The proof is complete.
∎

We have the following analog on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
:

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
N_{i}\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
N_{2}\ll N_{1}
,
N_{1}\gtrsim 1
,
L_{i}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
i=1,2
,
D^{*}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{N_{i},L_{i}}
,
i=1,2
with

\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta_{2}}{\xi_{2}}\big|\lesssim D^{*}

for
(\xi_{i},\eta_{i})\in\pi_{\xi,\eta}(\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}))
. Then the following estimate holds:

(29)

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim\log(D^{*})N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\max}/N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.

Proof.

First, we suppose that
L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
. In this case we apply Lemma
4.3
to find

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\max}N_{2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2},

which implies (
29
).

Next, suppose that
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
.
We carry out a Whitney decomposition in the transversality parameter

D\sim\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta_{2}}{\xi_{2}}\big|

in the range
D\in[\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},D^{*}]
:

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\leqslant\sum_{D\in\big[\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},D^{*}]}\sum_{I_{1}^{D}\sim I_{2}^{D}}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}^{I_{1}^{D}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}^{I_{2}^{D}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.

By the argument from the proof of Lemma
4.4
we can effectively localize the
\xi
-support to intervals of length
\mathcal{O}(\frac{D^{2}N_{2}}{N_{1}^{\alpha}}\big)
. We obtain for
D=\big(L_{\max}/N_{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
by applying Lemma
4.3

(30)

\|f^{I_{1}^{D}}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{I_{2}^{D}}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{L_{\max}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\langle L_{\max}N_{2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}^{I_{i}^{D}}\|_{L^{2}}.

This is sufficient for
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
.

Next, we handle the contribution
D\in\big(\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},N_{1}^{\alpha/2}\big]
. After localizing the
\xi
-support to intervals of length
\mathcal{O}\big(D^{2}N_{2}/N_{1}^{\alpha}\big)
by almost orthogonality, we can apply Proposition
4.1
to find

(31)

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{D\in\big(\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\big)]}\sum_{I_{1}^{D}\sim I_{2}^{D}}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}^{I_{1}^{D}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}^{I_{2}^{D}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{D\in\big(\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\big)]}\frac{DN_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\langle L_{\max}/D\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}\\
&\lesssim L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\max}/N_{1}^{\alpha/2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

Finally, we turn to the contribution with large transversality. Here we apply Proposition
4.1
directly to find

(32)

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{D\in[N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},D^{*}]}\sum_{I_{1}^{D}\sim I_{2}^{D}}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}^{I_{1}^{D}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}^{I_{2}^{D}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{D\in[N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},D^{*}]}\langle L_{\max}/D\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}\\
&\lesssim\log(D^{*})N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\max}/N_{1}^{\alpha/2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

Collecting contributions (
30
)-(
32
) the proof is complete.
∎

We record the following simple estimate, which is a consequence of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. This will serve as substitute when (
26
) fails:

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{D}^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfy support properties
\pi_{\xi}\text{supp}(f_{i})\subseteq I_{i}
,
i=1,2
with
|I_{i}|\sim N_{i}
and
\pi_{\eta}\text{supp}(f_{i})\subseteq J_{i}
with
|J_{i}|\lesssim M_{i}
and
|\tau_{i}-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{i},\eta_{i})|\lesssim L_{i}
for
(\tau_{i},\xi_{i},\eta_{i})\in\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})
and
i=1,2
.
The following estimate holds:

\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim(N_{\min}M_{\min}L_{\min})^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}.

4.2.
Nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequalities with measure

In preparation for the proof of trilinear convolution estimates for approximate solutions to dispersion-generalized KP-I equations, we prove Theorem
1.1
. We impose the following assumptions on the hypersurfaces
(S_{i})_{i=1,2,3}
following
[
25
,
27
]
:

Assumption 4.7
.

For
i=1,2,3
there exist
0<\beta\leqslant 1
,
b>0
,
A\geqslant 1
,
F_{i}\in C^{1,\beta}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
, where the
\mathcal{U}_{i}
denote open and convex sets in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
G_{i}\in O(3)
such that

(i)

the oriented surfaces
S_{i}
are given by

S_{i}=G_{i}\text{gr}(F_{i}),\quad\text{gr}(F_{i})=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:z=F_{i}(x,y),\;(x,y)\in\mathcal{U}_{i}\};

(ii)

the vector field
\mathfrak{n}_{i}:S_{i}\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
of outward unit normals on
S_{i}
satisfies
the Hölder condition:

\sup_{\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}}\frac{|\mathfrak{n}_{i}(\sigma)-\mathfrak{n}_{i}(\tilde{\sigma})|}{|\sigma-\tilde{\sigma}|^{\beta}}+\frac{|\mathfrak{n}_{i}(\sigma).(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})|}{|\sigma-\tilde{\sigma}|^{1+\beta}}\leqslant b;

(iii)

there is
A\geqslant 1
such that for all
\sigma_{i}\in S_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
the following estimate holds:

A^{-1}\leqslant|\mathfrak{n}_{1}(\sigma_{1})\wedge\mathfrak{n}_{2}(\sigma_{2})\wedge\mathfrak{n}_{3}(\sigma_{3})|\leqslant 1.

For convenience, we recall the statement of Theorem
1.1
:

Theorem 4.8

(Nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequalities with general measure)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
(S_{i})_{i=1,2,3}
be a collection of hypersurfaces, which satisfy Assumption
4.7
,
\nu_{\gamma}
be a Borel measure, which satisfies (
7
), and
f_{i}\in L^{2}(S_{i}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})
,
i=1,2
. Then the following estimate holds:

\|f_{1}*f_{2}\|_{L^{2}(S_{3}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}\lesssim A^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}(S_{i}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}.

Proof.

We follow the argument in
[
25
]
.
By duality it suffices to show the trilinear expression

\int_{S_{3}(\varepsilon)}(f_{1}*f_{2})f_{3}d\nu_{\gamma}\lesssim A^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}(S_{i}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}.

To this end, let
(B_{\varepsilon,j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be an essentially disjoint cover of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with balls of size
\varepsilon
. Set

J_{3,\varepsilon}=\{j\in\mathbb{N}:B_{\varepsilon,j}\cap S_{3}(\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset\}\text{ and }S_{3,j}(\varepsilon)=B_{\varepsilon,j}\cap S_{3}(\varepsilon).

By Minkowski’s inequality we find

\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(f_{1}*f_{2})(\lambda)f_{3}(\lambda)d\nu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\big|\leqslant\sum_{j\in J_{3,\varepsilon}}\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(f_{1}*f_{2})(\lambda)f_{3}\big|_{S_{3,j}(\varepsilon)}(\lambda)d\nu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\big|.

By Hölder’s inequality and (
7
), we find
