\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(f_{1}*f_{2})(\lambda)f_{3}\big|_{S_{3,j_{3}}(\varepsilon)}(\lambda)d\nu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\big|\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}(S_{i,j,\varepsilon})}.

Above we denote

\displaystyle S_{1,j,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\{\lambda_{1}\in S_{1}(\varepsilon):\exists\lambda^{\prime}\in B_{\varepsilon,j}:\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda_{1}\in S_{2}(\varepsilon)\},

\displaystyle S_{2,j,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\{\lambda_{2}\in S_{2}(\varepsilon):\exists\lambda^{\prime}\in B_{\varepsilon,j}:\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda_{2}\in S_{1}(\varepsilon)\}.

The following estimate was proved in
[
25
, Eq. (16), p. 13]
:

(33)

\sum_{j\in J_{3,\varepsilon}}\chi_{S_{1,j,\varepsilon}\times S_{2,j,\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\lesssim A.

With this at hand, we can conclude as follows:

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{j\in J_{3,\varepsilon}}\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(f_{1}*f_{2})(\lambda)f_{3}\big|_{S_{3,j}(\varepsilon)}(\lambda)d\nu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\big|\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\sum_{j\in J_{3,\varepsilon}}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{2}(S_{1,j,\varepsilon},d\nu_{\gamma})}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}(S_{2,j,\varepsilon},d\nu_{\gamma})}\|f_{3}\|_{L^{2}(S_{3,j}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\big(\sum_{j\in J_{3,\varepsilon}}\|f_{3}\|_{L^{2}(S_{3,j}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(\sum_{j\in J_{3,\varepsilon}}\|f_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S_{1,j,\varepsilon},d\nu_{\gamma})}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}(S_{2,j,\varepsilon},d\nu_{\gamma})}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|f_{3}\|_{L^{2}(S_{3}(\varepsilon),d\nu_{\gamma})}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\sum_{j\in J_{3,\varepsilon}}\chi_{S_{1,j,\varepsilon}\times S_{2,j,\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\\
&\quad\qquad\quad\times|f_{1}(\lambda_{1})|^{2}|f_{2}(\lambda_{2})|^{2}d\nu_{\gamma}(\lambda_{1})d\nu_{\gamma}(\lambda_{2})\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}A^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}(S_{i}(\varepsilon)}.\end{split}

∎

4.3.
Resonance and the nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequality

In addition to the lower bound for the difference in group velocity in the resonant case, we shall see that we can compute the full transversality.
To show a trilinear estimate in the resonant case, consider

\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau d\xi d\eta

with
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{N_{i},\leqslant L_{i}}
with
L_{i}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
. Let
N=\max(N_{i})
. We suppose that
N_{1}=N
and
N_{3}\sim N_{1}\gtrsim N_{2}
. We use the scaling

(34)

\tau\to\frac{\tau}{N^{\alpha+1}},\;\xi\to\frac{\xi}{N},\;\eta\to\frac{\eta}{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}

to reduce to
1\sim|\xi_{1}^{\prime}|\sim|\xi_{3}^{\prime}|\gtrsim|\xi_{2}^{\prime}|\sim\frac{N_{2}}{N_{1}}
. The smallness of
|\xi_{2}^{\prime}|
allows us to localize
\xi_{i}^{\prime}
to intervals of length
\frac{N_{2}}{N_{1}}
using almost orthogonality.

In the resonant case, it holds, moreover

\big|\frac{\eta_{1}^{\prime}}{\xi_{1}^{\prime}}-\frac{\eta_{2}^{\prime}}{\xi_{2}^{\prime}}\big|\sim 1.

This induces an almost orthogonal decomposition in
\eta_{i}^{\prime}/\xi_{i}^{\prime}
(cf.
[
21
]
), by which we can suppose that
\eta_{i}^{\prime}/\xi_{i}^{\prime}
is localized to intervals of size
\sim 1
. Now we use a Galilean invariance:

\frac{\eta_{i}^{\prime}}{\xi_{i}^{\prime}}\to\frac{\eta_{i}^{\prime}}{\xi_{i}^{\prime}}-A.

This localizes
|\frac{\eta_{i}^{\prime}}{\xi_{i}^{\prime}}|\lesssim 1
. Considering
i=2
, we can suppose that
|\eta_{2}^{\prime}|\lesssim N_{2}/N_{1}
. This induces an almost orthogonal decomposition in
\eta_{i}^{\prime}
, by which we can suppose that
(\xi_{i}^{\prime},\eta_{i}^{\prime})
are localized to balls of size
c(N_{2}/N_{1})
for some
c\ll 1
to be chosen later. We stress that like in the proof of Proposition
3.3
for
(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/(N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1})
, we have in general for the transformed variables
(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}-A\xi^{\prime})\notin\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/(N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1})
and some care is required on the cylinder when estimating the measure.

To compute the full transversality, we need to consider the wedge product of normals

|\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{3},\eta_{3})|.

We compute

\mathfrak{n}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=\begin{pmatrix}-(\alpha+1)|\xi^{\prime}|^{\alpha}+(\eta^{\prime})^{2}/(\xi^{\prime})^{2}\\
-2\eta^{\prime}/\xi^{\prime}\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

The normals have modulus
\sim 1
after rescaling and supposing that
|\eta^{\prime}|\lesssim 1
. This is clear for
\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{i}^{\prime},\eta_{i}^{\prime})
for
i=1,3
since
|\xi_{i}^{\prime}|\sim 1
. In the resonant case it holds moreover

\big|\frac{\eta_{1}^{\prime}}{\xi_{1}^{\prime}}-\frac{\eta_{2}^{\prime}}{\xi_{2}^{\prime}}\big|\sim 1,

for which reason we have
|\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime})|\sim 1
.

Let
C_{\alpha}=\alpha+1
. We find under the convolution constraint:

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\det(\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1}),\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2}),\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}))\\
&=\begin{vmatrix}-C_{\alpha}|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}}&-C_{\alpha}|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}^{2}}&-C_{\alpha}|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}+\frac{(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})^{2}}{(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{2}}\\
-\frac{2\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}&-\frac{2\eta_{2}}{\xi_{2}}&-\frac{2(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})}{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\\
1&1&1\end{vmatrix}\\
&=\frac{-2}{\Omega_{KdV}}\begin{vmatrix}-C_{\alpha}\xi_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{\xi_{1}}&-C_{\alpha}\xi_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}}&-C_{\alpha}|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})+\frac{(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})^{2}}{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\\
\eta_{1}&\eta_{2}&\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}\\
\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\end{vmatrix}.\end{split}

where
\Omega_{KdV}=\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})
. Now we subtract the first column and the second column from the third column to find

\begin{split}&\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{vmatrix}-C_{\alpha}\xi_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{\xi_{1}}&-C_{\alpha}\xi_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}}&-C_{\alpha}|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})+\frac{(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})^{2}}{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\\
\eta_{1}&\eta_{2}&\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}\\
\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\end{vmatrix}\\
&=\begin{vmatrix}-C_{\alpha}\xi_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{\xi_{1}}&-C_{\alpha}\xi_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}}&-C_{\alpha}\Omega_{\alpha,1}+\frac{(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})^{2}}{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}-\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}}\\
\eta_{1}&\eta_{2}&0\\
\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&0\end{vmatrix}\\
&=(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})(-C_{\alpha}\Omega_{\alpha,1}+\frac{(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})^{2}}{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}-\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}})\\
&=-(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})\big(C_{\alpha}\Omega_{\alpha,1}+\frac{(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})^{2}}{(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}\big).\end{split}

This can be summarized as:

\begin{split}&\quad|\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}_{1}+\eta_{2}^{\prime})|\\
&=\frac{2|\eta_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\eta_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{1}^{\prime}|}{|\xi_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime})|}\big|C_{\alpha}\Omega_{\alpha,1}(\xi^{\prime})+\frac{(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\eta_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime})}\big|.\end{split}

Note that
\Omega_{\alpha,1}
and
\frac{(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\eta_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime})}
have the same sign and by the resonance condition a comparable modulus:

|\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime}+\eta_{2}^{\prime})|\sim\frac{|\eta_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}-\eta_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{1}^{\prime}|}{|\xi_{1}^{\prime}\xi_{2}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime})|}\cdot\frac{N_{2}}{N_{1}}\sim\frac{N_{2}}{N_{1}}.

The extension off the convolution constraint for perturbations of size
c(N_{2}/N_{1})
is carried out by checking
|\partial_{\xi^{\prime}}\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{3}^{\prime},\eta_{3}^{\prime})|+|\partial_{\eta^{\prime}}\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{3}^{\prime},\eta_{3}^{\prime})|\lesssim 1
. This is immediate from
|\xi_{3}^{\prime}|\sim 1
and
|\eta_{3}^{\prime}|\lesssim 1
.

From
\mathfrak{n}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}))
the Hölder regularity assumption
4.7
(ii) with
\beta=1
follows. We can allow for crude estimates since the constant in the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney estimate does not depend on
b
and
\beta
.

Now we can formulate the trilinear estimate in the resonant case on different domains:

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\ni N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gtrsim N_{2}\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
L_{i}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, and
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{\alpha,N_{i},\leqslant L_{i}}
,
i=1,2,3
. Suppose that
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
. Then the following estimate holds:

(35)

\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau\big|\lesssim N_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3\alpha}{4}}N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Under the above assumptions, if
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/\gamma)
for some
\gamma\in(1/2,1]
, then the following estimate holds:

(36)

\big|\int\big(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\big)f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi(d\eta)_{\gamma}d\tau\big|\lesssim C(\underline{N},\underline{L})\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/\gamma)}

with

C(\underline{N},\underline{L})=\big(\frac{N_{1}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}(L_{\min}/N_{1}^{\alpha/2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\text{med}}/N_{1}^{\alpha/2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}(L_{\max}/N_{1}^{\alpha/2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

If
2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\ni N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gtrsim N_{2}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
L_{i}
like above, and
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\gamma)
with
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{\alpha,N_{i},\leqslant L_{i}}
,
i=1,2,3
. Then the following estimate holds:

(37)

\big|\int\big(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\big)f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}(d\xi)_{1}(d\eta)_{1}d\tau\big|\lesssim C(\underline{N},\underline{L})\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{iN_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L_{\xi,\eta}^{2}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\gamma)}.

with

C(\underline{N},\underline{L})=L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\text{med}}/N^{\alpha-1}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\max}/N^{\alpha-1}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark 4.10
.

The estimate (
35
) recovers
[
33
, Lemma 4.2]
. We shall be brief in the proof.
For
\alpha=2
, (
36
) recovers Robert’s estimate
[
32
, Proposition 5.7]
.
Moreover, (
37
) recovers the estimate of Zhang
[
36
]
.

Proof.

We start with the proof of the estimate in Euclidean space. To ease notation, let
N=N_{1}
. Firstly, we decompose
f_{i,j_{i},k_{i}}=\sum_{c}f^{c}_{i,j_{i},k_{i}}
into
\sim\langle L_{i}\rangle
functions, which are in the
1
-neighbourhood of a translation of the characteristic surface. Write below
g_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}=f^{c}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
to ease notation. We use the anisotropic rescaling to find

\begin{split}&\quad\int(g_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})g_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau d\xi d\eta\\
&=N^{2(\alpha+1)}N^{2}N^{\alpha+2}\int(g^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})g^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}.\end{split}

This makes the rescaled functions
f^{c}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
(or a translate thereof) supported in the
N^{-(\alpha+1)}
-neighbourhood of the characteristic surface. After the almost orthogonal decomposition from above, we can suppose that
|\eta_{i}^{\prime}|\lesssim 1
. We have computed for the transversality:

|\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime})\wedge\mathfrak{n}(\xi_{3}^{\prime},\eta_{3}^{\prime})|\sim\frac{N_{2}}{N_{1}}.

In the above
(\xi_{i}^{\prime},\eta_{i}^{\prime})
are contained in balls of size
c(N_{2}/N_{1})
, which constitute an almost orthogonal decomposition of the spatial frequency support.

Hence, we can apply Theorem
1.1
with Lebesgue measure and
\varepsilon=N^{-(\alpha+1)}
to find

|\int(g^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})g^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}|\lesssim\big(\frac{N_{1}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{-\frac{3(\alpha+1)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|g^{\prime}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}.

The claim follows from reversing the scaling (
34
), which yields a factor of

N^{-\frac{3(\alpha+1)}{2}}N^{-\frac{3}{2}}N^{-\frac{3(\alpha+2)}{4}}
, and summing over
c_{i}
with
\#c_{i}\sim L_{i}
with Cauchy-Schwarz. This finishes the proof in the Euclidean case.

We turn to the cylinder case.
First we suppose that
L_{\text{med}}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
. If
L_{\max}\leqslant N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
, we do not decompose. If
L_{\max}\geqslant N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
, we decompose
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
with
L_{i}=L_{\max}
into layers having modulation size
N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
such that in the following we suppose
L_{\max}\lesssim N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
. We use the anisotropic scaling

\tau\to\tau^{\prime}=\frac{\tau}{N_{1}^{\alpha+1}},\quad\xi\to\xi^{\prime}=\frac{\xi}{N},\quad\eta\to\eta^{\prime}=\frac{\eta}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}},

which leads to

\begin{split}&\quad\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/\gamma}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi(d\eta)d\tau\big|\\
&=N_{1}^{2}N_{1}^{2(\alpha+1)}\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/(\gamma N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1})}(f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}(d\eta^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\big|.\end{split}

Write
L_{i}^{\prime}=L_{i}/N_{1}^{\alpha+1}
. We carry out the Galilean transform

(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})=(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}-A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime})

with
A^{\prime}=N_{1}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}A
such that given
\xi^{\prime\prime}
we have
\eta^{\prime\prime}+A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}/N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}
. Moreover, we have
|\eta^{\prime\prime}|\lesssim 1
and we can carry out like above an almost orthogonal decomposition of
(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})
into balls of size
c(N_{2}/N_{1})
.

Next, we decompose the supports of
f^{\prime}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
into balls
\theta_{i}
of size
L_{\max}^{\prime}=\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{1}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim N_{1}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}
.

Then, we obtain

(38)

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{\theta_{3}}\big|\int\sum_{\theta_{1}\sim_{\theta_{3}}\theta_{2}}\big(f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,\theta_{2}}\big)f^{\prime}_{3,\theta_{3}}\big|\\
&\leqslant\sum_{\theta_{3}}\sum_{\theta_{1}\sim_{\theta_{3}}\theta_{2}}\|f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,\theta_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\|f^{\prime}_{3,\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

By symmetry we can suppose that
L_{1}^{\prime}=L_{\min}^{\prime}
. We estimate the convolution with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Let
I_{L_{\max}^{\prime}}
denote the interval of length
L^{\prime}_{\max}=L_{\max}/(N_{1}^{\alpha+1})
, which contains
\pi_{\eta^{\prime\prime}}(\text{supp}(f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}}))
. We let
J_{L_{\max}^{\prime}}
denote the interval which contains
\pi_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}(\text{supp}(f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}})
. We count the number of
\eta^{\prime}
such that
\eta^{\prime}-A^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}\in I_{L_{\max}^{\prime}}
for
\xi^{\prime\prime}\in I^{\prime}_{L_{\max}^{\prime}}
. This is similar to the proof of Proposition
3.3
:

\#\{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}/(N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}):(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\in I_{L_{\max}^{\prime}}\times I^{\prime}_{L_{\max}^{\prime}}\}\lesssim\langle A^{\prime}\cdot L^{\prime}_{\max}\cdot N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\rangle\sim\langle A^{\prime}\cdot\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\rangle.

Now, for fixed
\eta^{\prime}
from the above set, we estimate

\text{meas}(\{\xi^{\prime\prime}\in I_{L_{\max}^{\prime}}:(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\in I^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}_{\max}}\times I_{L_{\max}}\}\lesssim\frac{L^{\prime}_{\max}}{A^{\prime}}.

Given
(\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})
from above, we finally have

\text{meas}(\{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:(\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\in\theta_{1}\})\lesssim L^{\prime}_{\min}.

For this reason we find

\|f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,\theta_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\lesssim(L_{\min}^{\prime}(L^{\prime}_{\max}/A^{\prime})\langle A^{\prime}\cdot\frac{L_{\max}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\rangle)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\|f^{\prime}_{2,\theta_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}.

By two more applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to carry out the summation over
\theta_{i}
, we incur a factor of
A^{\frac{1}{2}}\sim\Big(\frac{N_{1}}{N_{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and obtain

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{\theta_{3}}\sum_{\theta_{1}\sim_{\theta_{3}}\theta_{2}}\|f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,\theta_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\|f^{\prime}_{3,\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\\
&\lesssim(L^{\prime}_{\min}L^{\prime}_{\max})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\theta_{3}}\sum_{\theta_{1}\sim_{\theta_{3}}\theta_{2}}\|f^{\prime}_{1,\theta_{1}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\|f^{\prime}_{2,\theta_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\|f^{\prime}_{3,\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}\\
&\lesssim(L^{\prime}_{\min}L_{\max}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}(N_{1}/N_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f^{\prime}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

Now we reverse the scaling which yields a factor
N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{2}}N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{2}(\alpha+1)}
. Taking into account the scaling factor from above gives

N_{1}^{2}N_{1}^{2(\alpha+1)}\big(\frac{N_{1}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{2}}N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{2}(\alpha+1)}N_{1}^{-(\alpha+1)}(L_{\min}L_{\max})^{\frac{1}{2}}=N_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}}N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(L_{\min}L_{\max})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This proves (
36
) in case
L_{\text{med}}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
.

We turn to the case
L_{\text{med}}\gtrsim N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
and suppose that
L_{\min}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
. We decompose the modulation of
f_{i_{j}}
with
L_{i_{1}}=L_{\text{med}}
and
L_{i_{2}}=L_{\text{max}}
into layers of thickness
N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, such that we can apply the previous result with
L_{\text{med}}\sim L_{\max}\sim N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
. The claim follows then from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality which incurs factors

(L_{\text{med}}/N_{1}^{\alpha/2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(L_{\text{max}}/N_{1}^{\alpha/2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Finally, we turn to the case
L_{\min}\gtrsim N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
. In this case we decompose all functions into layers of modulation with size
N_{1}^{\alpha/2}
. Then we can apply the previous arguments and finally, we can apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, which incurs a factor of

\big(\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}/N_{1}^{\frac{3\alpha}{2}}.

This completes the proof of the Loomis-Whitney inequality on the cylinder.

For the domain
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\gamma
we additionally use a bilinear Strichartz estimate as simple applications of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality do not seem to suffice; see Remark
4.11
.
Decompose the functions
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
into functions
g_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
with modulation of thickness
N_{1}^{\alpha-1}
such that after rescaling the functions have modulation
\lesssim N_{1}^{-2}
. Then it follows like in the proof of Theorem
1.1

(39)

\begin{split}&\quad\int(g_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})g_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau d\xi d\eta\\
&=N^{2(\alpha+1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/N\times\mathbb{Z}/N^{2}}(g^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})g^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\\
&\leqslant N^{2(\alpha+1)}\sum_{\theta_{3}}\|(g^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})_{\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\|g^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3},\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

Here
\theta_{3}
denote
N^{-1}
-balls.

We have by the bilinear Strichartz estimate in the resonant case and an almost orthogonal decomposition into
N^{-1}
-balls denoted by
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
:

\begin{split}&\quad\|(g^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})_{\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim(L_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+(L_{2}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}/2^{1/2})\sum_{\theta_{1}\sim_{\theta_{3}}\theta_{2}}\|g^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1},\theta_{1}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\|g^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2},\theta_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

Now we apply the Cauchy Schwarz inequality in
\theta_{3}
, which incurs a factor of
\langle L^{\prime}_{2}AN_{1}\rangle\sim 1
by
L^{\prime}_{2}\lesssim N_{2}N_{1}^{-2}
and
A\sim N_{1}/N_{2}
. For this reason we find with
L_{1}^{\prime}\lesssim N^{-2}
:

\begin{split}&\quad N^{2(\alpha+1)}\sum_{\theta_{3}}\|(g^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*g^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})_{\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\|g^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3},\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim(L_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{2\alpha+1}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|g^{\prime}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

Scaling back we obtain

N^{2\alpha+1}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|g^{\prime}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|g_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}.

Finally, we need to carry out the summation over thin modulation layers, which gives by the above

\eqref{eq:DecompositionLWPeriodic}\lesssim L_{\min}^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\text{med}}/N^{\alpha-1}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle L_{\max}/N^{\alpha-1}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{iN_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

On
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\gamma}
the decomposition to
\varepsilon=N^{-2}
balls does not recover Zhang’s estimate
[
36
]
. The computation is carried out for the KP-I equation for the sake of illustration.
We decompose
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}
into layers of modulation with thickness
N_{1}
such that after the anisotropic rescaling

\tau\to\tau/N^{3},\;\xi\to\xi/N,\;\eta\to\eta/N^{2}

we find functions with modulation
\lesssim N^{-2}=\varepsilon
. We compute

\begin{split}&\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\big(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau d\xi d\eta\\
&=N^{6}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/N\times\mathbb{Z}/N^{2}}(f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\tau d\xi d\eta\\
&=N^{6}\sum_{\theta_{3}:N^{-2}-\text{ball}}\int\big(f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})_{\theta_{3}}f^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3},\theta_{3}}d\tau^{\prime}d\xi^{\prime}d\eta^{\prime}\\
&\leqslant N^{6}\sum_{\theta_{3}}\|(f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})_{\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\|f^{\prime}_{3,N_{3},L_{3},\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

Applying Cauchy-Schwarz yields

\|(f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})_{\theta_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\lesssim N^{-1}\sum_{\theta_{1}\sim_{\theta_{3}}\theta_{2}}\|f^{\prime}_{1,N_{1},L_{1},\theta_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\|f^{\prime}_{2,N_{2},L_{2},\theta_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}.

In this case, the application of Cauchy-Schwarz only yields a factor
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}=N^{-1}
because we are dealing with two counting measures. The summation of
\theta_{3}
-balls incurs a factor of
A^{\frac{1}{2}}\sim\Big(\frac{N_{1}}{N_{2}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
such that

\begin{split}&\lesssim N^{6}N^{-1}\big(\frac{N_{1}}{N_{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f^{\prime}_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N^{6}N^{-1}\big(N_{1}/N_{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{-\frac{9}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\langle L_{i}/N\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}_{\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

This leaves a gap in the case of
N_{2}\ll N_{1}
compared to the result of Zhang
[
36
]
.

5.
Ill-posedness results for KP-I equations

In this section, we show that (
1
) posed on
\mathbb{D}\in\{\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},\mathbb{T}^{2}\}
is
C^{2}
ill-posed for different choices of
\alpha
, namely one cannot use Picard iteration (the fixed point argument) to solve the problem. On
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we prove the following optimal result, which improves on the argument from
[
33
]
. Moreover, we show that on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
for semilinear KP-I equations, the regularity for local well-posedness is strictly subcritical.

5.1.
Sharp
C^{2}
-illposedness for KP-I equations on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

We prove that (
68
) is not analytically well-posed in
H^{s_{1},s_{2}}
for
\alpha<\frac{5}{2}
.

First, we state a preliminary result:

Lemma 5.1
.

The spatial Fourier transform of

v(t)=\int_{0}^{t}U_{\alpha}(t-s)\partial_{x}(U_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{1}U_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{2})ds

is given by

(40)

\begin{split}\hat{v}(t,\xi,\eta)=\xi e^{it\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}\int_{*}\frac{1-e^{-it\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}}{\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})d\xi_{1}d\eta_{1},\end{split}

where
*
denotes the convolution constraint
(\xi,\eta)=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})+(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})
with
(\xi_{i},\eta_{i})\in D_{i}
.

Proof.

With
(\xi,\eta)=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})+(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}_{x,y}\Big(U_{\alpha}(t-s)\partial_{x}\big(U_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{1}U_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{2}\big)\Big)(t,\xi,\eta)\\
&~~=e^{i(t-s)\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}(i\xi)\mathcal{F}_{x,y}\big(U_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{1}U_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{2}\big)(t,\xi,\eta)\\
&~~=e^{i(t-s)\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}(i\xi)\int_{*}e^{is\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})}\hat{\phi}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})e^{is\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})}\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})d\xi_{1}d\eta_{1}\\
&~~=e^{it\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}(i\xi)\int_{*}e^{is(\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})+\omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta))}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})d\xi_{1}d\eta_{1}\\
&~~=i\xi e^{it\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}\int_{*}e^{-is\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})d\xi_{1}d\eta_{1}.\end{split}

This implies after integrating in time

\begin{split}\hat{v}(t,\xi,\eta)&=i\xi e^{it\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{*}e^{-is\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})d\xi_{1}d\eta_{1}ds\\
&=\xi e^{it\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}\int_{*}\frac{1-e^{-it\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}}{\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})d\xi_{1}d\eta_{1}.\end{split}

∎

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
1\leqslant\alpha<\frac{5}{2}
,
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, there does not exist a
T>0
such that there is a function space
X_{T}\hookrightarrow C([-T,T];H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}))
in which (
1
) admits a local solution with a
C^{2}
-differentiable flow-map
\Gamma_{t}
:

\Gamma_{t}:H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})\to H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}),\quad u_{0}\mapsto u(t),~~t\in[-T,T].

Proof.

We recall that the resonance function is given by

\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Omega_{\alpha}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})-\Omega_{\alpha}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}),

where

\begin{split}\Omega_{\alpha}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})&=|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})-|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}\xi_{1}-|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha}\xi_{2},\\
\Omega_{\alpha}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})&=\frac{(\eta_{1}\xi_{2}-\eta_{2}\xi_{1})^{2}}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}.\end{split}

We define functions via their Fourier transforms as follows:

(41)

\begin{split}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})&=\frac{1_{D_{1}}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})}{N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{4}}},\quad D_{1}=[N^{-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}},2N^{-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}]\times[-N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}},N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}],\\
\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})&=\frac{1_{D_{2}}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})}{N^{s_{1}+(1+\frac{\alpha}{2})s_{2}}N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{4}}},\\
D_{2}&=[N,N+N^{-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}]\times[\sqrt{1+\alpha}N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1},\sqrt{1+\alpha}N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}+N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}].\end{split}

From
[
33
, Lemma 3.1]
, we estimate the size of the resonance function by

|\Omega_{\alpha}|\sim 1.

In Lemma
5.1
we computed

|\hat{v}(t,\xi,\eta)|\sim t\frac{N}{N^{s_{1}}N^{(1+\frac{\alpha}{2})s_{2}}}

for a significant measure of
(\xi,\eta)=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})+(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})
with
(\xi_{i},\eta_{i})\in D_{i}
.
For
0<t\leqslant c\ll 1
, the Sobolev norm of
v(t)
is given by

\|v(t)\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\sim tN^{\frac{5}{4}-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

For
\Gamma_{t}
to be
C^{2}
-differentiable, it needs to hold

1\sim\|\phi_{1}\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\phi_{2}\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\gtrsim tN^{\frac{5}{4}-\frac{\alpha}{2}},

which requires
\alpha\geqslant\frac{5}{2}
for
N\gg 1
. Clearly,
\phi_{i}
are not real-valued, but letting
u^{*}_{0}=\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}
and symmetrizing the Fourier transform we find real-valued initial data
u_{0}
with comparable Sobolev norm. The above estimates remain unchanged, which completes the proof.
∎

5.2.
C^{2}
-illposedness for KP-I equations on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}

Next, we treat the case of the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\alpha<5
,
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then there does not exist any time
T>0
such that there is a function space
X_{T}\hookrightarrow C([-T,T];H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}))
in which (
1
) has a unique local solution with a
C^{2}
-differentiable flow map
\Gamma_{t}
:

\Gamma_{t}:H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})\to H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}),\quad u_{0}\mapsto u(t),~~t\in[-T,T].

We remark that the cases
\alpha\leqslant 1
can be readily seen to be
C^{2}
-illposed by comparison with the dispersion-generalized Benjamin-Ono equation:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u&=u\partial_{x}u,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},\\
u(0)&=u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}).\end{array}\right.

This evolution is recovered by considering initial data to KP-equations on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
, which do not depend on the periodic coordinate.

In the following we suppose that
\alpha\geqslant 1
. As an ansatz to prove Theorem
5.3
, we consider the following functions with parameters
\gamma
and
\beta(N)
to be determined:

(42)

\begin{split}\hat{\phi}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})&=\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}1_{D_{1}}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1}),~~D_{1}=[\gamma,2\gamma]\times\{0\},\\
\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})&=\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}N^{-s_{1}}(\beta(N))^{-s_{2}}1_{D_{2}}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2}),~~D_{2}=[N-\gamma,N]\times\{\beta(N)\},\end{split}

where
N\gg 1
,
\beta(N)\sim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}
, and
\gamma=\gamma(N)\ll 1
will be chosen later. We find an upper bound for the size of the resonance function. With
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}>0
, we can compute
\Omega_{\alpha}^{1}
invoking the mean value theorem:

\Omega_{\alpha}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\alpha+1}-\xi_{2}^{\alpha+1}-\xi_{1}^{\alpha+1}=(\alpha+1)\xi_{1}\xi_{*}^{\alpha}-\xi_{1}^{\alpha+1},

for
\xi_{*}\in(\xi_{2},\xi_{2}+\xi_{1})
. For
\eta_{1}=0
which is relevant for our setting, we have

\Omega_{\alpha}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}\xi_{1}}{\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}=:f(\xi_{1}).

Using Taylor’s theorem, we have

(43)

f(\xi_{1})=f(\xi_{1}^{*})+f^{\prime}(\xi_{1}^{*})(\xi_{1}-\xi_{1}^{*})+R,

where
R
includes all the lower order terms. This gives

f(\xi_{1})=f(\xi_{1}^{*})+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2})}(\xi_{1}-\xi_{1}^{*})-\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}(\xi_{1}^{*})}{\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2})^{2}}(\xi_{1}-\xi_{1}^{*})+R.

From the definition of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
in (
42
), we have that the first two terms on the right-hand side above have similar size while the remainder term is smaller in size than the first two terms. Now we choose
\xi_{1}^{*},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi^{*},\eta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the leading order terms in
\Omega_{1}^{\alpha}
and
\Omega_{2}^{\alpha}
cancel, namely

\begin{split}(\alpha+1)\xi_{1}\xi_{*}^{\alpha}-\xi_{1}^{\alpha+1}=f(\xi_{1}^{*})+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}(\xi_{1}-\xi_{1}^{*})}{\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2})}=\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}\xi_{1}}{\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2})},\end{split}

which gives

\eta_{2}^{2}=\big((\alpha+1)\xi_{*}^{\alpha}-\xi_{1}^{\alpha}\big)\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2}).

With this choice, for the size of the resonance function, we conclude

|\Omega_{\alpha}|\sim\Big|\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}\xi_{1}^{*}(\xi_{1}-\xi_{1}^{*})}{\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2})^{2}}\Big|\sim N^{\alpha-1}\gamma^{2}.

However, with the choice

\eta_{2}=:\beta(N,\gamma)=\Big(\big((\alpha+1)\xi_{*}^{\alpha}-\xi_{1}^{\alpha}\big)\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2})\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

we cannot ascertain that
\beta(N,\gamma)\in\mathbb{N}
. To this end, we find a rational approximation of
\eta_{2}
. Using Dirichlet’s approximation theorem, we have for
\beta(N,\gamma)\in\mathbb{R}
, the existence of infinitely many rational numbers
\frac{p}{q}
such that

(44)

|\beta(N,\gamma)-\frac{p}{q}|<\frac{1}{q^{2}}.

The above implies that

(45)

|q\beta(N,\gamma)-p|<\frac{1}{q}.

Since we have a natural number (
q\beta(N,\gamma)>0
) approximation for
q\beta(N,\gamma)
(and not
\beta(N,\gamma)
), we make use of the anisotropic scaling (
34
) for (
1
): for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}

\tau\to\lambda^{\alpha}\tau,\quad\xi\to\lambda\xi,\quad\eta\to\lambda^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}\eta.

With this, for
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the following scaling

(46)

\tau\to\tau^{\prime}:=q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}\tau,\quad\xi\to\xi^{\prime}:=q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}\xi,\quad\eta\to\eta^{\prime}:=q\eta.

It is easy to check that with the scaling (
46
), the resonance function scales as

\Omega_{\alpha}\to\Omega^{\prime}_{\alpha}=q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}\Omega_{\alpha}.

Now we work with the scaled variables (
\xi^{\prime}_{i},\eta^{\prime}_{i})
,
i=1,2
and ensure that the natural number approximation (
45
) of
q\beta(N,\gamma)
does not affect the size of the resonance function. Consider the difference

\begin{split}|\Omega^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime}_{1},\xi^{\prime}_{2},0,q\beta(N,\gamma))-\Omega^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime}_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime},0,p)|&=q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}|\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},0,\beta)-\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},0,\frac{p}{q})|\\
&=q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}\frac{\xi_{1}}{\xi_{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}\Big|\beta^{2}-\frac{p^{2}}{q^{2}}\Big|\\
&\lesssim q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}\frac{\gamma}{N^{2}}\Big|\beta-\frac{p}{q}\Big|\Big|\beta+\frac{p}{q}\Big|\\
&\lesssim q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}\frac{\gamma}{N^{2}}\frac{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}{q^{2}}.\end{split}

We require the size of the above expression to be negligible compared to

|\Omega^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\xi^{\prime}_{1},\xi_{2}^{\prime},0,q\beta(N,\gamma))|
, i.e.,

q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}\frac{\gamma}{N^{2}}\frac{N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}}{q^{2}}\ll q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}N^{\alpha-1}\gamma^{2}

which requires

(47)

q\gg N^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Furthermore, we choose
\gamma=\gamma(N)
such that the size of the new resonance function
\Omega^{\prime}_{\alpha}
is small, namely

q^{2\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}N^{\alpha-1}\gamma^{2}\sim 1,

which gives

(48)

\gamma\sim N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}}q^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{\alpha+2}}.

We remark that this is consistent with (
47
) since taking the two conditions together

q^{\frac{\alpha+3}{2(\alpha+2)}}\gg N^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

This is automatically satisfied for
\alpha\geqslant 1
,
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, and
q\in\mathbb{N}
.

With the choice of
q
and
\gamma
made above, we are set to prove Theorem
5.3
.

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

We define the input functions
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
via their spatial Fourier transforms as follows:

\begin{split}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})&=q^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+2}}\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}1_{D_{1}}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1}),\text{ where }D_{1}=[q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}\gamma,2q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}\gamma]\times\{0\},\\
\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})&=q^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+2}}\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}N)^{-s_{1}}p^{-s_{2}}1_{D_{2}}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2}),\\
&\quad\text{ where }D_{2}=[q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}(N-\gamma),q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}N]\times\{p\}.\end{split}

In the above definition we choose
q
such that
[q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}\gamma,2q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}\gamma]\subseteq[0,1]
, i.e.,
q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}\gamma\lesssim 1
which follows from (
48
) and
\alpha\geqslant 1
.
It is straight-forward to check that
\|\phi_{i}\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}\sim 1
for
i=1,2
. We consider the contribution to the second Picard iterate given by the Duhamel integral:

v(t)=\int_{0}^{t}S_{\alpha}(t-s)\partial_{x}\big(S_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{1}S_{\alpha}(s)\phi_{2}\big)ds.

To show that the flow map
\Gamma_{t}
is not
C^{2}
differentiable at the origin, we show that

\|v(t)\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}\to\infty\text{ as }N\to\infty.

From Lemma
5.1
, we have

\hat{v}(t,\xi,\eta)=\xi e^{it\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)}\int_{*}\frac{1-e^{-it\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}}{\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})d\xi_{1}d\eta_{1},

where
*
denotes the convolution constraint and we have the counting measure in the
\eta
variable. To compute the
H^{s_{1},s_{2}}
norm of
v
, we estimate the size of
\hat{v}
for
0<t\leqslant c\ll 1
:

|\hat{v}(t,\xi,\eta)|\sim t\frac{q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}N}{(q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}N)^{s_{1}}p^{s_{2}}}.

Thus,

\|v(t)\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}^{2}\sim\int\langle\xi\rangle^{2s_{1}}\langle\eta\rangle^{2s_{2}}|\hat{v}(t,\xi,\eta)|^{2}d\xi d\eta\sim t^{2}q^{\frac{4}{\alpha+2}}N^{2}q^{\frac{2}{\alpha+2}}\gamma.

For the flow map
\Gamma_{t}
to be
C^{2}
, we require

1\sim\|\phi_{1}\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}\|\phi_{2}\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}\gtrsim\|v(t)\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}\sim tq^{\frac{3}{\alpha+2}}N\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\sim tN^{-\frac{1}{4}\frac{5-\alpha}{\alpha+3}}N^{\frac{5-\alpha}{4}},

where we used (
47
) and (
48
) to obtain the last term in the above display. Hence, we conclude that
\Gamma_{t}
can be
C^{2}
-differentiable only for
\alpha\geqslant 5
.
∎

5.3.
Subcritical illposedness for semilinear KP-I equations on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}

We supplement the analytic well-posedness result proved in Theorem
9.3
:

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\alpha>5
, and
s<\frac{1-\alpha}{4}
. Then, for initial data in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
, (
1
) is ill-posed on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
, i.e., the data-to-solution map

u_{0}\mapsto u(t)

fails to be continuous.

Proof.

The proof relies on
[
3
, Proposition 1]
. The starting point is an analytic data-to-solution mapping assigning more regular initial data from a ball in
H^{\bar{s},0}
,
\bar{s}\in(\frac{1-\alpha}{4},0)
to solutions of higher regularity:

(B_{R,\bar{s}},\|\cdot\|_{H^{\bar{s},0}})\to(B_{R},X_{\bar{s}}),\quad u_{0}\mapsto u.

This is provided by Theorem
9.3
.

[
3
, Proposition 1]
now states that
a possible well-posedness for
s^{\prime}<\frac{1-\alpha}{4}
implies continuity of

\begin{split}(B_{R,\bar{s}},\|\cdot\|_{H^{s^{\prime},0}})\to C_{T}H^{s^{\prime},0},\quad u_{0}&\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}S_{\alpha}(t-s)(\partial_{x}(S_{\alpha}(s)u_{0}S_{\alpha}(s)u_{0}))ds.\end{split}

The above display is the second Picard iterate, and we furnish initial data for which the estimate

(49)

\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}S_{\alpha}(t-s)(\partial_{x}(S_{\alpha}(s)u_{0}S_{\alpha}(s)u_{0}))ds\Big\|_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})}^{2}

holds only for
s\geqslant\frac{1-\alpha}{4}
. We rely on a High
\times
High
\to
Low resonant interaction, which is crucial to apply
[
3
, Proposition 1]
because the sequence of initial data must be contained in
(B_{R,\bar{s}},\|\cdot\|_{H^{\bar{s},0}})
. Define

\hat{u}_{0}(\xi,\eta)=1_{[A,A+A^{1-\alpha}]}(\xi)\cdot\delta_{0}(\eta)+1_{[1-A,1-A+A^{1-\alpha}]}(\xi)\cdot\delta_{m}(\eta),

where
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\ll A\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is such that for some output frequencies
\xi
, we have
\xi\sim 1
. It is straightforward to check that

\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s,0}}\sim A^{s}A^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}.

Again we remark that
u_{0}
is not real-valued, which can be accomplished by symmetrization of the Fourier transform like in previous sections.

Furthermore, choosing
m\sim A^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
and
A
suitably, we have

|\Omega_{\alpha}(\xi_{1},\xi-\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\eta-\eta_{1})|\sim 1.

Indeed, it suffices to check this for one specific
\xi_{1}\in A
and
\xi\sim 1
. The estimate follows then from simple derivative estimates.

By Lemma
5.1
and that
|\xi|\sim 1
we have

\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}S_{\alpha}(t-s)(\partial_{x}(S_{\alpha}(s)u_{0}S_{\alpha}(s)u_{0}))ds\Big\|_{H^{s,0}}\sim N^{\frac{3-3\alpha}{2}}.

For (
49
) to hold, we require

N^{\frac{3-3\alpha}{2}}\lesssim N^{2s}N^{1-\alpha},

which is true only for
s\geqslant\frac{1-\alpha}{4}
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.4.
Ill-posedness on tori

Finally, we prove the following result on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, there is
\gamma=\gamma(\alpha)\in(1/2,1]
such that there does not exist any time
T>0
, for which there is a function space
X_{T}\hookrightarrow C([-T,T];H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}))
in which (
1
) has a unique local solution with a
C^{2}
-differentiable flow map
\Gamma_{t}
:

\Gamma_{t}:H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\gamma})\to H^{s_{1},s_{2}}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}),\quad u_{0}\mapsto u(t),~~t\in[-T,T].

Proof.

Considering initial data, which do not depend on the second variable, the evolution becomes

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u=u\partial_{x}u.

For this equation it is easy to see, choosing initial data

\hat{u}_{0}(\xi)=\delta_{1}(\xi)+\delta_{N}(\xi)

for
N\gg 1
, the data-to-solution mapping fails to be
C^{2}
for
\alpha<1
.

In the following we suppose that
\alpha\geqslant 1
and
consider the following functions:

(50)

\begin{split}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})&=1_{D_{1}}(\xi_{1},\eta_{1}),\quad D_{1}=\{1\}\times\{0\},\\
\hat{\phi}_{2}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})&=N^{-s_{1}}p^{-s_{2}}1_{D_{2}}(\xi_{2},\eta_{2}),\quad D_{2}=\{N\}\times\{p\}.\end{split}

We choose
p
such that

\Omega_{\alpha}(1,0,N,p)=(N+1)^{\alpha+1}-1-N^{\alpha+1}-\frac{p^{2}}{(N+1)N}=0.

Note that this requires a choice of
\gamma\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
such that
p\in\gamma^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, i.e., fixing the ratio of the periods.

Clearly, for the Fourier transform of the Duhamel integral

|\hat{v}(t,\xi,\eta)|\sim\frac{Nt}{N^{s_{1}}p^{s_{2}}},\quad\xi=N+1,\;\eta=p.

Hence,

\|v(t)\|_{H^{s_{1},s_{2}}}\gtrsim Nt.

This implies that for
\alpha\geqslant 1
, the data-to-solution mapping of (
1
) fails to be
C^{2}
for some
\gamma=\gamma(\alpha)
.

∎

6.
Improved quasilinear local well-posedness for the KP-I equation on the Euclidean plane

In this section we prove the following result on local well-posedness for the KP-I equation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(51)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u-\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}^{2}u&=u\partial_{x}u,\quad(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
u(0)&=u_{0}\in H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).\end{array}\right.

Theorem 6.1
.

(
51
) is locally well-posed provided that
s>\frac{1}{2}
in the following sense: The data-to-solution mapping
S_{T}^{\infty}:H^{\infty,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to C_{T}H^{\infty,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
extends continuously to
S_{T}^{s}:H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to C_{T}H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
T=T(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})
depending lower semicontinuously on the initial data and
T(u)\gtrsim 1
as
u\downarrow 0
.

We begin with short-time bilinear estimates stated in Proposition
6.2
and then show energy estimates in short-time function spaces in Proposition
6.3
. We choose the frequency-dependent time localization
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
, which is chosen to precisely ameliorate the derivative loss in the nonlinear estimate. Moreover, we consider Sobolev spaces with low frequency weight to estimate the differences of solutions:

\|f\|_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+|\xi|^{-1})^{1+2\delta}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}|\hat{f}(\xi,\eta)|^{2}d\xi d\eta.

\delta
is chosen depending on
s
:

\delta=\frac{s-\frac{1}{2}}{2}\wedge\frac{1}{8}.

The choice of frequency-dependent time localization and low frequency weights can be traced back to the work of Ionescu–Kenig–Tataru
[
21
]
.
Short-time function spaces to estimate the difference of the solution with low frequency weight are denoted by

\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
,
\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

6.1.
Outline of the proof.

The general argument can be found already in
[
21
]
, so we shall be brief.
Solutions
u
to (
51
) are iterated in short-time function spaces as follows with
T\in(0,1]
,
s\geqslant s^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\|u\|_{F^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|u\|_{B^{s}(T)}+\|\partial_{x}(u^{2})\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)},\\
\|\partial_{x}(u^{2})\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|u\|_{F^{s}(T)}\|u\|_{F^{s^{\prime}}(T)},\\
\|u\|^{2}_{B^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|u(0)\|^{2}_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|u\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)}\|u\|_{F^{s^{\prime}}(T)}.\end{array}\right.

By a standard bootstrap argument this implies a priori estimates

\|u\|_{F^{s}(1)}\lesssim\|u(0)\|_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

for
s\geqslant s^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}
only depending on smallness of the
H^{s^{\prime},0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-norm. And by subcriticality of this norm, smallness can always be accomplished by rescaling.

Differences of solutions
v=u_{1}-u_{2}
with
u_{i}
solutions to (
51
) are propagated as follows with
s>\frac{1}{2}
and
T\in(0,1]
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}&\lesssim\|v\|_{\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}+\|\partial_{x}(v(u_{1}+u_{2}))\|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)},\\
\|\partial_{x}(v(u_{1}+u_{2}))\|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}&\lesssim\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s}(T)}),\\
\|v\|^{2}_{\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}&\lesssim\|v(0)\|_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}^{2}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s}(T)}).\end{array}\right.

For small initial data this implies by limiting properties of the function spaces as
T\downarrow 0
and another continuity argument

\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1)}\lesssim\|v(0)\|_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}.

We conclude local well-posedness from invoking the Bona-Smith argument. Differences of solutions in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
are estimated as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\|v\|_{F^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|v\|_{B^{s}(T)}+\|\partial_{x}(v(u_{1}+u_{2}))\|_{F^{s}(T)},\\
\|\partial_{x}(v(u_{1}+u_{2}))\|_{\mathcal{N}^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|v\|_{F^{s}(T)}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s}(T)}),\\
\|v\|^{2}_{B^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|v(0)\|^{2}_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|v\|^{3}_{F^{s}(T)}\\
&\quad+\|v\|_{F^{s}(T)}\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(T)}.\end{array}\right.

Consider
u_{0}\in H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the frequency-truncation
P_{\leqslant H}u_{0}
. The solution to (
51
) emanating from
P_{\leqslant H}u_{0}
is denoted by
u^{H}
.

We have for the difference of the solutions
v=u-u^{H}
:

\begin{split}\|v\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|v(0)\|^{2}_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|v\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)}(\|u\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u^{H}\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)})\\
&\quad+\|v\|^{3}_{F^{s}(T)}+\|v\|_{F^{s}(T)}\|v(0)\|_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\|u^{H}(0)\|_{F^{2s+\frac{1}{2}}(T)}.\end{split}

Now we plug in the a priori estimates obtained previously to find

\begin{split}\|v\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)}&\lesssim\|v(0)\|^{2}_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|v\|^{2}_{F^{s}(T)}(\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|u^{H}(0)\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{1}{2},0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})\\
&\quad+\|v\|^{3}_{F^{s}(T)}+\|v\|_{F^{s}(T)}\|P_{\geqslant H}u(0)\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2},0}}\|u^{H}(0)\|_{H^{2s+\frac{1}{2},0}}.\end{split}

This implies for small enough initial data in
H^{s,0}
:

\|u-u^{H}\|_{F^{s}(T)}\lesssim\|P_{\geqslant H}u(0)\|_{H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Next, consider
u_{0n}\to u_{0}
in
H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We expand the difference of solutions:

(52)

\|u_{n}-u\|_{C_{T}H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leqslant\|u_{n}-u_{n}^{H}\|_{C_{T}H^{s,0}}+\|u_{n}^{H}-u^{H}\|_{C_{T}H^{s,0}}+\|u_{n}^{H}-u\|_{C_{T}H^{s,0}}.

By the above, the first and third term is estimated as

\|u_{n}-u_{n}^{H}\|_{C_{T}H^{s,0}}+\|u_{n}^{H}-u\|_{C_{T}H^{s,0}}\lesssim\|u_{0n}^{\geqslant H}\|_{H^{s,0}}+\|u_{0}^{\geqslant H}\|_{H^{s,0}}.

We can choose
H
large enough such that for
n\geqslant n_{0}

\|u_{0n}^{\geqslant H}\|_{H^{s,0}}+\|u_{0}^{\geqslant H}\|_{H^{s,0}}\leqslant\delta.

Consequently, by the local well-posedness of (
51
) in
H^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon,0}
(see e.g.
[
31
, Section 8]
), which is used to estimate the center term in (
52
), we find

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}-u\|_{C_{T}H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\delta.

Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary, the proof of local well-posedness is complete.

6.2.
Short-time bilinear estimates

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\alpha=2
, and
r_{2}\geqslant r_{1}>\frac{1}{2}
. Then, the following estimate holds:

(53)

\|\partial_{x}(u_{1}u_{2})\|_{\mathcal{N}^{r_{2}}(T)}\lesssim\|u_{1}\|_{F^{r_{1}}(T)}\|u_{2}\|_{F^{r_{2}}(T)}+\|u_{1}\|_{F^{r_{2}}(T)}\|u_{2}\|_{F^{r_{1}}(T)}.

Secondly, for
s>\frac{1}{2}
the following estimate holds:

(54)

\|\partial_{x}(uv)\|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\lesssim\|u\|_{F^{s}(T)}\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}.

Proof.

By definition of the function spaces, the claim reduces to proving summable estimates:

(55)

\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(P_{N_{1}}u_{1}P_{N_{2}}u_{2})\|_{\mathcal{N}_{N}(T)}\lesssim C(N,N_{1},N_{2})\|P_{N_{1}}u_{1}\|_{F_{N_{1}}(T)}\|P_{N_{2}}u_{2}\|_{F_{N_{2}}(T)}.

In the following, we reduce to estimates for functions localized in modulation taking into account the time localization. The arguments are standard (cf.
[
21
,
34
]
), and we shall be brief.

6.2.1.
Low
\times
High
\to
High interaction (
N_{2}\ll N_{1}\sim N
)

In the following we consider extensions
P_{N_{i}}\tilde{u}_{i}
of
P_{N_{i}}u_{i}
such that

\|P_{N_{i}}\tilde{u}_{i}\|_{F_{N_{i}}}\leqslant 2\|P_{N_{i}}u_{i}\|_{F_{N_{i}}(T)}.

To lighten notations, we shall again denote
\tilde{u}_{i}\to u_{i}
.

We use the definition of the
\mathcal{N}_{N}
-norm to bound the left hand side of (
55
) by

\begin{split}&\sup_{t_{N}\in\mathbb{R}}\|(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)+iN)^{-1}\xi 1_{A_{N}}(\xi)\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[u_{1,N_{1}}\cdot\eta_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))]\\
&\quad*\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[u_{2,N_{2}}\cdot\eta_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))]\|_{X_{N}}.\end{split}

We define

f_{i,N_{i}}=\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[u_{i,N_{i}}\cdot\eta_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))].

Consider
L_{1},L_{2}\geqslant N
and
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and for
L_{i}=N
in
D_{N,\leqslant L}
. By the function space properties it suffices to obtain an estimate

(56)

\sum_{L\geqslant N}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}N\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim(1\vee N_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(N_{2}\wedge 1)^{\varepsilon}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.

Once the above display is established, estimates (
55
) and consequently (
53
) and (
54
) follow from summation and properties of the function spaces. Note that by the time localization we have
L,L_{i}\geqslant N
. Below we shall distinguish the cases of

(1)

Very low frequencies:
N\lesssim 1
,

(2)

Resonant case:
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
,

(3)

Non-resonant case:
L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
.

(1) Very low frequencies

N\sim N_{1}\lesssim 1
. We carry out an additional homogeneous frequency decomposition. In this case we apply the bilinear Strichartz estimate from Lemma
4.3
to find

\begin{split}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}&\lesssim(L_{1}\wedge L_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(L_{1}\vee L_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}N_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

(2) Resonant case

N\gg 1
,
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
. If
N_{2}\sim 1
, we carry out an additional homogeneous frequency decomposition. Recall that in the resonant case, we have the transversality bound

\big|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\xi_{1}}-\frac{\eta-\eta_{1}}{\xi-\xi_{1}}\big|\sim N,

which allows for the bilinear Strichartz estimate invoking Proposition
4.1
:

\begin{split}\sum_{N\leqslant L\ll N_{1}^{2}N_{2}}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}&\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

This implies (
56
) for
r_{i}
,
s>\frac{1}{2}
.

(3) Non-resonant case.

N\gg 1
,
L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
. If
N_{2}\sim 1
, we carry out an additional homogeneous dyadic decomposition in
N_{2}\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
. We distinguish between
L=L_{\max}
and
L_{i}=L_{\max}
. In case
L=L_{\max}
we obtain for
N_{2}\gtrsim N_{1}^{-1}
by applying the bilinear Strichartz estimate in Lemma
4.3

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{L\geqslant N_{1}^{2}N_{2}}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim(N_{1}^{2}N_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}}(L_{1}\wedge L_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(L_{1}\vee L_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}(N_{2}/N_{1})^{\frac{1}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

This implies (
53
) and (
54
) for
r_{i},s\geqslant 0
.

For
N_{2}\ll N_{1}^{-1}
the same argument gives

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{L\geqslant N_{1}}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}}N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.\end{split}

This shows (
53
) and (
54
) for
r_{i},s>-\frac{1}{2}
.
The Low-High-High-interaction is settled.

6.2.2.
High
\times
High
\to
High interaction

N\sim N_{1}\sim N_{2}\gg 1
. We distinguish between resonant case
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{3}
and non-resonant case
L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{3}
.

(1) Resonant case

L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{3}
. In this case, we can apply the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality (
35
) after invoking duality to find

\sum_{N_{1}\leqslant L\ll N_{1}^{3}}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log(N_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.

This shows (
53
) and (
54
) for
r_{i},s>-\frac{1}{2}
.

(2) Non-resonant case

L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{3}
. Suppose by symmetry (up to logarithmic loss) that
L=L_{\max}
. In this case we apply Hölder’s inequality and two
L^{4}
-Strichartz estimates from Proposition
3.1
:

\sum_{L\gtrsim N_{1}^{3}}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}.

This implies again (
53
) and (
54
) for
r_{i},s>-\frac{1}{2}
.

6.2.3.
High
\times
High
\to
Low interaction

N\ll N_{1}\sim N_{2}
. If
N\lesssim 1
, we carry out an additional homogeneous frequency decomposition in
N
. In the following we suppose that
N_{1}\gg 1
as the case
N\lesssim N_{1}\lesssim 1
can be handled like above via
L^{4}
-Strichartz estimates from Proposition
3.1
. The reduction to modulation localized estimates differs from the previous cases because the time localization of
\mathcal{N}_{N}
-norm does not suffice to estimate the
F_{N_{i}}
-norm. We add time-localization, which incurs a factor of
N_{1}(1\vee N)^{-1}
:

\begin{split}&\quad\|(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)+iN)^{-1}\xi 1_{A_{N}}(\xi)\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[u_{1,N_{1}}u_{2,N_{2}}\cdot\eta^{2}_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))]\|_{X_{N}}\\
&=\|(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)+iN)^{-1}\xi 1_{A_{N}}(\xi)\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[u_{1,N_{1}}\eta_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))\\
&\quad\cdot u_{2,N_{2}}\eta_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\gamma^{2}(N_{1}t-k)]\|_{X_{N}}.\end{split}

Consequently, it suffices to estimate
N_{1}(1\vee N)^{-1}
terms of the form

\begin{split}&\quad\|(\tau-\omega_{\alpha}(\xi,\eta)+iN)^{-1}\xi 1_{A_{N}}(\xi)\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[u_{1,N_{1}}\gamma(N_{1}t-k)\eta_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))]\\
&\quad*\mathcal{F}_{t,x,y}[u_{2,N_{2}}\gamma(N_{1}t-k)\eta_{0}(N(t-t_{N}))]\|_{X_{N}}.\end{split}

(1) Very small frequencies
(
N\lesssim N_{1}\lesssim 1
): As mentioned above, this case can be handled using two
L^{4}
Strichartz estimates as in the previous estimate.

The case
N\lesssim 1\lesssim N_{1}\sim N_{2}
is considered in the following cases.

(2) Resonant case
(
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{2}N
): In this case, we apply the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality (
35
) to find

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{N\leqslant L\ll N_{1}^{2}N}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log(N_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

This implies (
53
) for
r_{2}\geq r_{1}>0
taking into account derivative loss and time localization.

For (
54
) we need to handle the low frequency weight. The above estimate suffices for
s>\frac{1}{2}
when
N\gtrsim N_{1}^{-2}
. If
N\ll N_{1}^{-2}
we apply Lemma
4.3
to find

(57)

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{N\leqslant L\ll N_{1}^{2}N}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim N^{\frac{3}{4}}\log(N_{1})N_{1}^{-\frac{3}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

This shows (
54
) also for very low
N\ll 1
.

(3) Non-resonant case

L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{2}N
. Suppose that
L=L_{\max}
. The other cases only deviate logarithmically by additional summation in the modulation. We apply two
L^{4}
-Strichartz estimates from Proposition
3.1
to find

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{L\gtrsim N_{1}^{2}N}L^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|1_{D_{N,L}}(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

This shows (
53
) and (
54
) for
r_{i},s>1/2
and
N\gg 1
.

Next, we suppose that
N\ll 1\ll N_{1}\sim N_{2}
. We carry out an additional homogeneous decomposition in frequency
N\in 2^{-\mathbb{N}}
to localize the derivative. The above display suffices for (
53
).
As long as
N\gtrsim N_{1}^{-2}
the above display still suffices for (
54
). If
N\ll N_{1}^{-2}
, we apply a bilinear Strichartz estimate from Lemma
4.3
to find (
57
), which also finishes the proof of (
54
). The proof of Proposition
6.2
complete.
∎

6.3.
Short-time energy estimates

Next, we turn to the iteration of the energy norm. We show the following:

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
T\in(0,1]
,
s_{1}\geqslant s_{2}>\frac{1}{2}
, and
u\in C([0,T],H^{\infty,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
be a smooth solution to (
1
) with
\alpha=2
. Then the following estimate holds:

(58)

\|u\|^{2}_{B^{s_{1}}(T)}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s_{1},0}}^{2}+\|u\|_{F^{s_{1}}(T)}^{2}\|u\|_{F^{s_{2}}(T)}.

Let
s>\frac{1}{2}
, and
v=u_{1}-u_{2}
for
u_{i}\in C([0,T],H^{\infty,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Then the following estimates hold:

(59)

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v(0)\|_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|v\|^{2}_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s}(T)}),

(60)

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{B^{s}(T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v(0)\|_{H^{s,0}}^{2}+\|v\|^{3}_{F^{s}(T)}+\|v\|_{F^{s}(T)}\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\|u_{2}\|_{F^{2s+\frac{1}{2}}(T)}.

Proof.

We begin with the proof of (
58
).
We invoke the fundamental theorem of calculus to write for
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
N\gg 1
and
t\in[0,T]
:

(61)

\|P_{N}u(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=\|P_{N}u(0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}u\partial_{x}(P_{N}(u^{2}))dxds.

To obtain more favorable estimates, we integrate by parts to assign the derivative always to the lowest frequency term. The arguments are standard, and we shall be brief (cf.
[
21
,
34
,
33
]
).

We use a paraproduct decomposition:

P_{N}(u^{2})=2P_{N}(uP_{\ll N}u)+P_{N}(P_{\gtrsim N}uP_{\gtrsim N}u).

We decompose

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}(uP_{\ll N}u)\partial_{x}P_{N}udxds=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\ll N,\\
K\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}(uP_{K}u)\partial_{x}P_{N}udxds.

Write

P_{N}(uP_{K}u)=P_{N}uP_{K}u+[P_{N}(uP_{K}u)-P_{N}uP_{K}u].

For the first term we can integrate by parts to assign the derivative on the lowest frequency:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}uP_{K}u(\partial_{x}P_{N}u)dx=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(P_{N}u)^{2}(\partial_{x}P_{K}u)dx.

For the second term we consider the bilinear Fourier multiplier:

m(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})[\chi_{N}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})\chi_{K}(\xi_{2})-\chi_{N}(\xi_{1})\chi_{K}(\xi_{2})].

It is a consequence of the mean-value theorem that

\big|\partial_{\xi_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}m(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big|\lesssim KN^{-\alpha_{1}}K^{-\alpha_{2}}\tilde{\chi}_{N}(\xi_{1})\tilde{\chi}_{K}(\xi_{2})

for suitable mild enlargements of
\chi_{N}
,
\chi_{K}
. Consequently, by Fourier series expansion the second term can effectively be regarded as

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big[P_{N}(uP_{K}u)-P_{N}uP_{K}u\big]\partial_{x}P_{N}udxds\sim\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{P}_{N}uP_{N}u\partial_{x}P_{K}udxds.

The reduction points out that we can always regard the derivative acting on the lowest frequency.

\bullet
High
\times
Low
\to
High interaction (
N_{2}\ll N_{1}\sim N_{3}
): We show the estimate

(62)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}N_{2}\ll N_{1}\sim N_{3},\\
N_{1}\gtrsim 1\end{subarray}}N_{1}^{2s_{1}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N_{1}}u\partial_{x}P_{N_{2}}uP_{N_{3}}udxds\lesssim\|u\|^{2}_{F^{s_{1}}(T)}\|u\|_{F^{s_{2}}(T)}.

Firstly we smoothly decompose the interval
[0,t]\subseteq[0,T]
into intervals of length
\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}
:

1=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\gamma^{3}(N_{1}t-k)

for a suitable
\gamma\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
. This achieves the required time-localization to carry out estimates in the short-time Fourier restriction spaces. In the following we focus on estimates

(63)

\begin{split}&\quad\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\gamma(N_{1}s-k)P_{N_{1}}u)(\gamma(N_{1}s-k)P_{N_{2}}u)(\gamma(N_{1}s-k)P_{N_{3}}u)dxds\big|\\
&\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|P_{N_{i}}u\|_{F_{N_{i}}}.\end{split}

Estimates (
62
) follow from taking into account time localization, which incurs a factor
TN_{1}
and the derivative loss, which draws a factor
N_{2}
.
2
2

2

We remark that the boundary terms actually require a separate estimate due to the sharp time-cutoff. There are at most
4
terms, which effectively gains a factor
N^{-1}
from the time localization. We refer to the literature for further details (cf.
[
21
]
).

We turn to establishing (
63
): By an application of Plancherel’s theorem and the properties of the function spaces, it suffices to establish estimates

(64)

\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}

for
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{N_{i},L_{i}}
,
L_{i}\geqslant N_{1}
. We record this in the following lemma which is proved in
[
21
]
. We include the proof for self-containedness.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
N_{1}\sim N_{2}\gtrsim N_{3}
for
N_{1}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
N_{3}\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then (
64
) holds true.

Proof.

First, we handle the resonant case:
\max_{i}(L_{i})\ll N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
. An application of the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality (
35
) gives

\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2},

which is (
64
).

Next, suppose we are in the non-resonant case:
\max(L)\gtrsim N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
. Suppose that
L_{1}=L_{\max}
. The other cases can be handled similarly. Applying Hölder’s inequality and two
L^{4}_{t,x,y}
-Strichartz estimates from Proposition
3.1
yields again (
64
):

\begin{split}\big|\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau\big|&\leqslant\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\prod_{i=2}^{3}\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}[f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}]\|_{L^{4}_{t,x,y}}\\
&\lesssim(N_{1}^{2}N_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{L_{\tau,\xi,\eta}^{2}}.\end{split}

∎

\bullet
High
\times
High
\to
High interaction (
N_{1}\sim N_{2}\sim N_{3}\gg 1
): The estimate (
63
) follows again from applying Lemma
6.4
.

\bullet
High
\times
High
\to
Low interaction (
N_{1}\ll N_{2}\sim N_{3}
): We obtain from the above reductions and an application of Lemma
6.4

\begin{split}&\quad\sum_{1\lesssim N_{1}\ll N_{2}\sim N_{3}}N_{1}^{2s_{1}}\big|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_{x}P_{N_{1}}u)P_{N_{2}}uP_{N_{3}}udxds\big|\\
&\lesssim\sum_{1\lesssim N_{1}\ll N_{2}\sim N_{3}}N_{1}^{2s_{1}}N_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|P_{N_{i}}u\|_{F_{N_{i}}}.\end{split}

Dyadic summation using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality implies (
58
) for the claimed regularities. This finishes the proof of (
58
).

\hfill\Box

6.4.
Energy estimates for the difference of solutions

We turn to the proof of (
59
). Invoking the fundamental theorem of calculus for a solution to the difference equation on frequencies
N\gg 1
yields

\|P_{N}v(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|P_{N}v(0)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}v\partial_{x}(P_{N}(v(u_{1}+u_{2})))dxds.

We take again a paraproduct decomposition:

(65)

\begin{split}P_{N}(v(u_{1}+u_{2}))&=P_{N}(vP_{\ll N}(u_{1}+u_{2}))+P_{N}(P_{\gtrsim N}vP_{\gtrsim N}(u_{1}+u_{2}))\\
&\quad+P_{N}(P_{\ll N}v(u_{1}+u_{2})).\end{split}

The first term can be handled by integration by parts like above. The second term is different because we need to estimate one function with high frequency at negative Sobolev regularity. It suffices to show for
i=1,2
and
s>\frac{1}{2}
:

\sum_{1\lesssim N_{1}\lesssim N_{2}\sim N_{3}}N_{1}^{-1}\big|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_{x}P_{N_{1}}v)P_{N_{2}}vP_{N_{3}}u_{i}dxds\big|\lesssim\|v\|^{2}_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\|u_{i}\|_{F^{s}(T)}.

This is reduced to dyadic estimates for
1\lesssim N_{1}\lesssim N_{2}\sim N_{3}
:

(66)

\big|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N_{1}}vP_{N_{2}}vP_{N_{3}}u_{i}dxds\big|\lesssim N_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|P_{N_{1}}v\|_{F_{N_{1}}}\|P_{N_{2}}v\|_{F_{N_{2}}}\|P_{N_{3}}u_{i}\|_{F_{N_{3}}}

for extensions
v
,
u_{i}
. Time localization incurs a factor
N_{2}
, by which we reduce to the convolution estimate (after changing to Fourier variables, modulation localization, and taking into account the properties of the function spaces):

\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau\lesssim N_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{2}^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}

with
1\lesssim N_{1}\lesssim N_{2}\sim N_{3}
and
L_{i}\geqslant N_{2}
. This is the content of Lemma
6.4
.

The third term is different because the derivative does not act on the lowest frequency and we cannot integrate by parts due to lack of symmetry. Here the estimate at negative regularity comes to rescue. We need to obtain summable estimates for
K\ll N
,
N\gg 1
:

\big|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}vP_{K}v\tilde{P}_{N}u_{i}dxds\big|\lesssim K^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|P_{N}v\|_{F_{N}}\|P_{K}v\|_{F_{K}}\|\tilde{P}_{N}u_{i}\|_{F_{N}}.

Note that we have not included the factor coming from time localization.

For
K\gtrsim 1
the above estimate has already been verified in (
66
).

Suppose that
K\lesssim 1
. Let
N_{1}\sim N_{3}\gtrsim N_{2}\sim K
. In the resonant case
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
we can use a bilinear Strichartz estimate from Proposition
4.2
to find with above notations:

\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.

In the non-resonant case
L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{2}N_{2}
we can use a different bilinear Strichartz estimate Lemma
4.3
on
f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}
to find with
L_{2}=L_{\max}
:

\begin{split}\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau&\lesssim(N_{1}^{2}N_{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}N_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}}N_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}\\
&\lesssim N_{1}^{-1}N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

This estimate is acceptable. Applying the analog argument for
L_{i}=L_{\max}
,
i\in\{1,3\}
gives a better estimate.
With this estimate we find by dyadic summation for any
s>\frac{1}{2}
:

(67)

\big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\ll N,\\
N\gtrsim 1\end{subarray}}N^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}v\partial_{x}(P_{K}v\tilde{P}_{N}(u_{1}+u_{2}))dxds\big|\lesssim\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}^{2}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{s}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{s}(T)}).

This finishes the proof of (
59
).

We turn to the proof of (
60
). To this end, we rewrite

\begin{split}&\quad\|P_{N}v(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
&=\|P_{N}v(0)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}v\partial_{x}(P_{N}(v(u_{1}+u_{2})))dxds\\
&=\|P_{N}v(0)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}v\partial_{x}(P_{N}(v^{2}))dxds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}v\partial_{x}(P_{N}(vu_{2}))dxds.\end{split}

The estimate of the first integral in the last line is like for (
58
). For the second integral we carry out a paraproduct decomposition like in (
65
):

P_{N}(v\,u_{2})=P_{N}(v\,P_{\ll N}u_{2})+P_{N}(P_{\gtrsim N}vP_{\gtrsim N}u_{2})+P_{N}(P_{\ll N}v\,u_{2}).

The first term can be handled by integration by parts, the second term can likewise be treated like in the estimate (
58
). For the last term we use the argument from (
67
) to find for
s>\frac{1}{2}
:

\big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\ll N,\\
N\gtrsim 1\end{subarray}}N^{2s}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{N}v\partial_{x}(P_{K}v\tilde{P}_{N}u_{2})dxds\big|\lesssim\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\|v\|_{F^{s}(T)}\|u_{2}\|_{F^{2s+\frac{1}{2}}(T)}.

The proof of (
60
) is complete.
∎

7.
Improved quasilinear local well-posedness for the dispersion-generalized KP-I equation on the Euclidean plane

Next, we show improved local well-posedness for quasilinear fractional KP-I equations with
\alpha\in(2,5)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(68)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}D_{x}^{\alpha}u-\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}^{2}u&=u\partial_{x}u,\quad(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
u(0)&=u_{0}\in H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\end{array}\right.

The second and third author showed local well-posedness for
s(\alpha,\varepsilon)\geqslant 5-2\alpha+\varepsilon
in
[
33
]
. Here we improve the result as follows: Define

(69)

s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})=\begin{cases}6-\frac{11\alpha}{4}+\varepsilon,&\quad\alpha\in(2,\frac{24}{11}],\\
0,&\quad\alpha\in(\frac{24}{11},\frac{5}{2}),\end{cases}

In
[
33
]
we showed a priori estimates for
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
. In the following we show local well-posedness for
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(2,\frac{5}{2})
. Then (
68
) is locally well-posed provided that
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
\varepsilon>0
. The data-to-solution mapping
S_{T}^{\infty}:H^{\infty,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to C_{T}H^{\infty,0}
extends to a mapping
S_{T}^{s}:H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to C_{T}H^{s,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
T=T(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s,0}})
depending lower semicontinuously on
\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s,0}}
and
T(u)\gtrsim 1
as
u\downarrow 0
.

7.1.
Outline of the proof.

We use again short-time Fourier restriction like in Section
6
. The present choice of frequency-dependent time localization is like in
[
33
]
:
T=T(N)=N^{-(5-2\alpha+\varepsilon)}
. This interpolates between
T=T(N)=N^{-1}
for the KP-I equation (
51
) and semilinear local well-posedness for (
68
) with
\alpha=\frac{5}{2}
, which will be established in Section
9
. There is a significant difference between the KP-I equation and the dispersion-generalized KP-I equations: The nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality recovers more than one derivative in the resonant case and a logarithmic loss from dyadically summing the modulation can be compensated. This makes the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality also useful for the short-time bilinear estimate.

Presently, we improve the regularity by estimating the differences of solutions in
H^{-\frac{1}{2},0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and use a weight for the low frequencies:

\|f\|^{2}_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},0}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+|\xi|^{-1})^{1+2\varepsilon}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}|\hat{f}(\xi,\eta)|^{2}d\xi d\eta.

We denote the derived short-time function spaces with low frequency weight as
\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)
or
\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)
, respectively.

We show the following short-time bilinear estimates:

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
T\in(0,1]
, and
\alpha\in(2,\frac{5}{2})
. There is
\varepsilon>0
such that the following estimate holds for
r_{1}\geqslant 0
:

(70)

\|\partial_{x}(uv)\|_{\mathcal{N}^{r_{1}}(T)}\lesssim\|u\|_{F^{r_{1}}(T)}\|v\|_{F^{0}(T)}+\|u\|_{F^{0}(T)}\|v\|_{F^{r_{1}}(T)}.

Secondly, the following estimate holds for
r\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,2\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

(71)

\|\partial_{x}(uv)\|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\lesssim\|u\|_{F^{r}(T)}\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}.

Remark 7.3
.

We presently remark on the choice of parameters:
\varepsilon
in the time localization is chosen small enough such that (
70
) holds (cf.
[
33
, Proposition 5.2]
). We choose the low frequency weight with the same
\varepsilon
. This allows us to quantify the well-posedness for
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,5\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

The energy estimates in short-time Fourier restriction norms read as follows:

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
T\in(0,1]
, and
\alpha\in(2,\frac{5}{2})
. Let
u\in C([0,T];H^{\infty,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
be a smooth solution to (
68
). For
r\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,5\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
the following estimate holds:

(72)

\|u\|^{2}_{B^{r}(T)}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{H^{r,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+\|u\|^{2}_{F^{r}(T)}\|u\|_{F^{s_{1}}(T)}.

Let
v=u_{1}-u_{2}
be a difference of two smooth solutions to (
68
). For
r\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
the following estimates hold:

(73)

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{\bar{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v(0)\|^{2}_{\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|v\|^{2}_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}(\|u_{1}\|_{F^{r}(T)}+\|u_{2}\|_{F^{r}(T)}),

(74)

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{B^{r}(T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v(0)\|^{2}_{H^{r,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|v\|^{3}_{F^{r}(T)}+\|v\|_{\bar{F}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\|v\|_{F^{r}(T)}\|u_{2}\|_{F^{2r+\frac{1}{2}}(T)}.

The estimates for solutions were proved in
[
33
]
: (
70
) was proved in
[
33
, Proposition 5.2]
, and (
82
) was proved in
[
33
, Proposition 5.8]
. The proof of Theorem
6.1
can be carried out in three steps:

•

Proof of a priori estimates for
s\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon)
, which has already been carried out in
[
33
]
,

•

Proof of Lipschitz continuous dependence in
\bar{H}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for solutions in
F^{r}(T)
,
r\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

•

Conclusion of local well-posedness via Bona–Smith approximation.

The second and third step were described in the context of the KP-I equation in Section
6
. We presently omit the details to avoid repetition.

7.2.
A trilinear convolution estimate

The following trilinear estimate will play a central role in establishing the short-time estimates.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\alpha\geqslant 2
,
N_{i}\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
with
N_{2}\ll N_{1}\sim N_{3}
,
N_{1}\gtrsim 1
, and
L_{i}\gtrsim(1\vee N_{i}^{5-2\alpha+\varepsilon})
. Let
f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
with
\text{supp}(f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}})\subseteq D_{N_{i},L_{i}}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then the following estimate holds:

(75)

\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau\lesssim N_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3\alpha}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.

Proof.

In the resonant case
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
this is a consequence of the nonlinear Loomis-Whitney inequality (
35
). In case
L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
we distinguish between
L_{2}=L_{\max}
and
(L_{1}=L_{\max}
or
L_{3}=L_{\max})
.

In case
L_{2}=L_{\max}
we apply Hölder’s inequality and two
L^{4}_{t,x,y}
-Strichartz estimates from Proposition
3.1
to find

\begin{split}\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau&\leqslant\|f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\prod_{i=1,3}\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}[f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}]\|_{L^{4}_{t,x,y}}\\
&\lesssim(N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{1}^{\frac{2-\alpha}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2},\end{split}

which is (
75
).

Next, suppose
L_{3}=L_{\max}\gtrsim N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2}
. We apply Hölder’s inequality and Lemma
4.4
to find

\begin{split}\int(f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}})f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}d\xi d\eta d\tau&\leqslant\|f_{3,N_{3},L_{3}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\|f_{1,N_{1},L_{1}}*f_{2,N_{2},L_{2}}\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi,\eta}}\\
&\lesssim(N_{1}^{\alpha}N_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{N_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N_{1}^{\frac{\alpha}{4}}}\prod_{i=1}^{3}L_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i,N_{i},L_{i}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

This completes the proof.
∎

7.3.
Short-time bilinear estimates

In this section we finish the proof of Proposition
7.2
by showing (
71
).

As usually, the claim follows from estimates for dyadically localized frequencies:

\|P_{N}\partial_{x}(P_{N_{1}}uP_{N_{2}}v)\|_{\mathcal{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(T)}\lesssim C(N,N_{1},N_{2})\|P_{N_{1}}u\|_{F_{N_{1}}(T)}\|P_{N_{2}}v\|_{F_{N_{2}}(T)}

for
N,N_{1},N_{2}\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
. For
N\gtrsim N_{2}
we can use the estimates from the proof of (
72
).

It remains to check the
High
\times
High
\to
Low
interaction. In this case the necessity to estimate the high frequency at low regularity leads to the condition
r\geqslant s_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon,\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We shall consider the following cases:

(1)

Very low frequencies,

(2)

Resonant case,

(3)

Non-resonant case.

(1) Very small frequencies
(
N\lesssim 1\ll N_{1}
): We consider two subcases:

(i)
L_{\max}\ll N_{1}^{\alpha}N
: Since
(N\vee 1)=1
